terms Investments in associates and joint ventures Overview This chapter addresses the requirements of IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures. IAS 28 requires that investments in entities over which an investor has significant influence are classified as associates and are generally accounted for using the equity method of accounting. Joint arrangements that are classified as joint ventures are also generally accounted for using the equity method as described in IAS 28. The classification of joint arrangements as either joint ventures or joint operations is required by IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements and is discussed in Chapter 13 at section 5. The term joint venture is often used colloquially with different meanings, but references to ‘joint venture’ in this chapter have the meaning as defined in IAS 28. The equity method of accounting involves a modified form of consolidation of the results and assets of investees in the investor’s financial statements. The essence of the equity method of accounting is that, rather than full scale consolidation on a line-by-line basis, it requires incorporation of the investor’s share (adjusted for basis differences, like fair value adjustments arising on initial acquisition of the interest in the associate or joint venture) of the investee’s: • Net assets, in one line in the investor’s consolidated statement of financial position • Profit or loss, in one line in the investor’s consolidated statement of profit or loss • Other comprehensive income (OCI), in one line in the investor’s consolidated statement of other comprehensive income Since its issuance, IAS 28 has been subject to several amendments. The equity method of accounting is the subject of a standard-setting project of the International Accounting Standards Board (IASB), as discussed in section 13 below. What you need to know • An investor has significant influence over an associate when it has the power to participate in the financial and operating policy decisions of the investee but does not have control or joint control of those policies. • In the investor’s consolidated financial statements, associates and joint ventures are accounted for using the equity method, unless one of the exemptions in IAS 28 applies. • Under the equity method, an associate or joint venture is first recognised at its cost, including any directly attributable expenditures. • The carrying amount is increased or decreased to recognise the investor’s share of the profit or loss of the associate or joint venture after the date of acquisition, adjusted for basis differences. • Generally, equity method losses are recognised until the investor’s interest in the associate or joint venture is reduced to zero. • The equity method is discontinued when the investment ceases to be an associate or joint venture. • In the investor’s separate financial statements, there is an accounting policy choice for the measurement of associates and joint ventures between cost, in accordance with IFRS 9 Financial Instruments or using the equity method. Investments in associates and joint ventures Introduction 1.1 Objective of IAS 28 The objective of the standard is to prescribe the accounting for investments in associates and to set out the requirements for the application of the equity method when accounting for investments in associates and joint ventures. [IAS 28.1]. IAS 27 Separate Financial Statements allows an entity, in its separate financial statements, to account for its investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates using the equity method of accounting as described in IAS 28. [IAS 27.10]. This is discussed further in Chapter 9 at section 2.3. 1.2 Scope of IAS 28 The standard is applied by all entities that are investors with joint control of a joint venture, or significant influence over an associate. [IAS 28.2]. Although there are no exemptions from the standard itself, there are exemptions from applying the equity method by certain types of entities as discussed in section 3 below. 1.3 Definitions The following terms are used in IAS 28 with the meanings specified: [IAS 28.3] IAS 28 also notes that the following terms are defined in IAS 27.4 and in Appendix A of IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements and are used in IAS 28, with the meanings specified in the IFRS accounting standard in which they are defined: • Control of an investee • Group • Parent • Separate financial statements And • Subsidiary [IAS 28.4] Extract from IAS 28 The following terms are used in this Standard with the meanings specified: An associate is an entity over which the investor has significant influence. Consolidated financial statements are the financial statements of a group in which assets, liabilities, equity, income, expenses and cash flows of the parent and its subsidiaries are presented as those of a single economic entity. The equity method is a method of accounting whereby the investment is initially recognised at cost and adjusted thereafter for the post-acquisition change in the investor’s share of the investee’s net assets. The investor’s profit or loss includes its share of the investee’s profit or loss and the investor’s other comprehensive income includes its share of the investee’s other comprehensive income. A joint arrangement is an arrangement of which two or more parties have joint control. Joint control is the contractually agreed sharing of control of an arrangement, which exists only when decisions about the relevant activities require the unanimous consent of the parties sharing control. A joint venture is a joint arrangement whereby the parties that have joint control of the arrangement have rights to the net assets of the arrangement. A joint venturer is a party to a joint venture that has joint control of that joint venture. Significant influence is the power to participate in the financial and operating policy decisions of the investee but is not control or joint control of those policies. Investments in associates and joint ventures 1.4 Key differences between IAS 28 and ASC 323 IAS 28 is not converged with its US GAAP equivalent, ASC 323 Investments - Equity Method and Joint Ventures. However, they are similar, as summarised below, and therefore key differences are highlighted in this section. This is not an exhaustive list of differences. Further detail can be found in the US GAAP/IFRS accounting differences identifier tool.1 • An equity investment that gives an investor significant influence over an investee is considered an equity method investment under both US GAAP and IFRS accounting standards. • An investor is generally presumed to have significant influence when it holds 20% or more of the voting interest in an investee. • The equity method of accounting for such investments generally is consistent under US GAAP and IFRS accounting standards. FASB differences The key differences between the IFRS accounting standards and US GAAP guidance on this topic are as follows: • When determining significant influence under IAS 28, potential voting rights are considered if currently exercisable. Under US GAAP, potential voting rights are generally not considered. • Under IAS 28, the equity method is applied when an investor has significant influence, regardless of the legal form of the investee. Under US GAAP, the equity method is also applied when an investor holds more than 3-5% of a limited partnership or similar entity, unless the limited partner has virtually no influence over the investee, or the investor meets the criteria to apply proportional amortisation and elects that approach. • Investments in associates held by venture capital organisations, mutual funds, unit trusts and similar entities are exempt from using the equity method under IAS 28 and the investor can elect to measure its investments in these associates at fair value. Under US GAAP (via ASC 825-10 Financial Instruments), all entities have the option to account for equity method investments at fair value. Therefore, the scope of entities that can apply a fair value measurement model is different. • Uniform accounting policies between the investor and investee are required when applying IAS 28. Conforming accounting policies between investor and investee is generally not permitted under US GAAP. US GAAP/IFRS accounting differences identifier tool, EY, December 2024, available on ey.com. Investments in associates and joint ventures Significant influence An investor has significant influence over an associate when it has the power to participate in the financial and operating policy decisions of the investee but does not have control or joint control of those policies. [IAS 28.3]. Under IAS 28, a holding of 20% or more of the voting power of the investee (held directly or indirectly, through subsidiaries, see section 2.3 below) is presumed to give rise to significant influence, unless it can be clearly demonstrated that this is not the case. Conversely, a holding of less than 20% of the voting power is presumed not to give rise to significant influence, unless it can be clearly demonstrated that there is in fact significant influence. The existence of a substantial or majority interest of another investor in the investee does not necessarily preclude the investor from having significant influence. [IAS 28.5]. How we see it In our view, an entity considers both ordinary shares and other categories of shares in determining its voting rights, because IAS 28 focuses on ‘voting power’. Extract from IAS 28 The existence of significant influence by an entity is usually evidenced in one or more of the following ways: (a) representation on the board of directors or equivalent governing body of the investee; (b) participation in policy-making processes, including participation in decisions about dividends or other distributions; (c) material transactions between the entity and its investee; (d) interchange of managerial personnel; or (e) provision of essential technical information. **[IAS 28.6].** Significant influence might also exist over another entity through potential voting rights (see section 2.4 below). [IAS 28.7]. An entity loses significant influence over an associate when it loses the power to participate in the financial and operating policy decisions of that associate. The loss of significant influence can occur with or without a change in absolute or relative ownership levels. It could result from a contractual arrangement. It could also occur, for example, when an associate becomes subject to the control of a government, court, administrator or regulator. [IAS 28.9]. An entity reassesses whether it has the ability to exercise significant influence when facts and circumstances change. The accounting for loss of significant influence over an associate is discussed in section 9 below. 2.1 Lack of significant influence Indicators that the presumption of significant influence has been overcome include the following2: • The investor has tried and failed to obtain representation on the investee’s board of directors. • The investee, other shareholders or regulatory authorities are opposing the investor’s attempts to exercise significant influence. • The investor and investee sign an agreement under which the investor surrenders significant rights as a shareholder, such as a standstill agreement. • The investor is unable to obtain timely or adequate financial information required to apply the equity method. Or • A group of shareholders that holds the majority ownership of the investee operates without regard to the views of the investor. Similar considerations to those included in US GAAP, ASC 323-10-15-6. ## Contents - 3. SIGNIFICANT ACCOUNTING JUDGEMENTS AND ESTIMATES [extract] Investments in associates and joint ventures How we see it Determining whether the presumption of significant influence has been overcome requires considerable judgement. In our experience, many regulators take a keen interest in these decisions. IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities requires that an entity must disclose significant judgements and assumptions made in determining that it does not have significant influence even though it holds 20% or more of the voting rights of another entity. [IFRS 12.9(d)]. This is discussed further in Chapter 14 at section 3. 2.2 Holdings of less than 20% of the voting power Although there is a presumption that an investor that holds less than 20% of the voting power in an investee does not have significant influence, [IAS 28.5], careful judgement is needed to assess whether significant influence exists if one of the indicators in IAS 28.6(a) to (e) are present (see section 2 above). For example, an investor might still be able to exercise significant influence in the following circumstances: • The investor’s voting power is much larger than that of any other shareholder of the investee. • The corporate governance arrangements could be such that the investor is able to appoint members to the board, supervisory board or significant committees of the investee. The investor will need to apply judgement to the facts and circumstances to determine whether representation on the respective boards or committees is enough to provide significant influence. Or • The investor has the power to veto significant financial and operating decisions. How we see it Determining whether the indicators above result in an investor having significant influence despite a holding of less than 20% of the voting power requires considerable judgement. IFRS 12 requires that an entity must disclose significant judgements and assumptions made in determining that it does have significant influence when it holds less than 20% of the voting rights of another entity. [IFRS 12.9(e)]. This is discussed further in Chapter 14 at section 3. Practical example 2-1 below shows Aveng Limited’s disclosure of significant influence when it has voting rights of less than 20%. Practical example 2-1: Aveng Limited (2021) South Africa Audited consolidated annual financial statements 2021 [extract] Notes to the consolidated financial statements [extract] ### Judgements and estimation assumptions [extract] #### Equity-accounted investments [extract] … 2.3 Assessing significant influence when an investor has both a direct holding and an indirect holding through an associate or joint venture Extract from IAS 28 If an entity holds, directly or indirectly (eg through subsidiaries), 20 per cent or more of the voting power of the investee, it is presumed that the entity has significant influence, unless it can be clearly demonstrated that this is not the case. Conversely, if the entity holds, directly or indirectly (eg through subsidiaries), less than 20 per cent of the voting power of the investee, it is presumed that the entity does not have significant influence, unless such influence can be clearly demonstrated. A substantial or majority ownership by another investor does not necessarily preclude an entity from having significant influence. **[IAS 28.5].** Investments in associates and joint ventures The reference to investments held ‘directly or indirectly (e.g., through subsidiaries)’ is not explicitly clear whether investments held through an associate are considered when making the assessment of significant influence over an entity. Further, IAS 28.27 is clear that when assessing an investor’s share of an associate or joint venture for the purposes of applying equity accounting, holdings of the group’s other associates or joint ventures are ignored for this purpose. This raises a question of how to account for a direct interest in a subsidiary of an associate or joint venture (that does not on its own give the investor significant influence or control). Considered alone, the direct interest would be a financial asset accounted for under IFRS 9, but considered in the context of the investment in the associate or joint venture would be accounted for under IAS 28. How we see it As with any assessment of whether an investor has significant influence over another entity, the determination in this case requires judgement. Accounting for the direct interest depends on facts and circumstances. If the investor can demonstrate significant influence over the subsidiary of its associate or joint venture (“the investee”), the direct interest is accounted for under IAS 28. If not, it is a financial asset. If the investor concludes that it has significant influence over the investee, applying IAS 28.27, its share of the investee is limited to the direct interest since holdings of associates and joint ventures are ignored in determining the investor’s share in an associate or joint venture. The indirect interest in the investee is accounted for via equity accounting for the associate or joint venture. Illustration 2-1: Investor holding a direct and indirect interest in an investee Company A owns 30% of Company B and has significant influence over it. B has a 90% subsidiary, Company C, in which A also has a direct 8% interest. A’s direct and indirect equity interests provide it with certain rights over C and there are no other relevant agreements providing rights over C. A has subsidiaries and prepares consolidated financial statements. How should A account for its 8% direct interest in C in its consolidated financial statements? A considers the facts and circumstances of its relationship with C, including the factors in IAS 28.6 (see section 2 above) for the determination of significant influence. If the facts and circumstances indicate that A has significant influence over C, A equity accounts for its 8% interest in C and a further 27% indirect interest is equity accounted through its interest in B. If the facts and circumstances indicate that A does not have significant influence over C, the direct interest is accounted for as a financial asset under IFRS 9 and the 27% interest is accounted for under IAS 28 via A’s equity accounting for B. 2.4 Potential voting rights Extract from IAS 28 An entity may own share warrants, share call options, debt or equity instruments that are convertible into ordinary shares, or other similar instruments that have the potential, if exercised or converted, to give the entity additional voting power or to reduce another party’s voting power over the financial and operating policies of another entity (ie potential voting rights). The existence and effect of potential voting rights that are currently exercisable or convertible, including potential voting rights held by other entities, are considered when assessing whether an entity has significant influence. Potential voting rights are not currently exercisable or convertible when, for example, they cannot be exercised or converted until a future date or until the occurrence of a future event. **[IAS 28.7].** Investments in associates and joint ventures In assessing whether potential voting rights contribute to significant influence, an entity must examine facts and circumstances that affect potential voting rights, including their terms of exercise and any other contractual arrangements, whether considered individually or in combination. However, an entity does not include in the assessment the intention of management and the financial ability to exercise or convert those potential voting rights. [IAS 28.8]. IAS 28 does not include guidance on potential voting rights comparable to that included in IFRS 10 (see # Chapter 7 at section 4.3.4). In the amendments introduced to IAS 28 when IFRS 10, IFRS 11 and IFRS 12 were issued, the IASB did not reconsider the definition of significant influence and concluded that it would not be appropriate to address one element of the definition in isolation. Any such consideration would be done as part of a wider review of accounting for associates. [IAS 28.BC16]. Therefore, potential voting rights that are currently exercisable are included in the assessment of significant influence even if they are not ‘substantive’ in terms of IFRS 10. An example is a currently exercisable call option that is out of the money. 2.5 Fund managers At its November 2016 meeting, the IFRS IC discussed a request to clarify whether, and how, a fund manager assesses if it has significant influence over a fund that it manages and in which it has a direct investment but does not control. The IFRS IC observed that a fund manager assesses whether it has control, joint control or significant influence over a fund that it manages by applying the relevant IFRS accounting standard, which in the case of significant influence, is IAS 28. Unlike IFRS 10, IAS 28 does not contemplate whether and how decision-making authority held in the capacity of an agent affects the assessment of significant influence. The IFRS IC decided not to add the issue to its agenda.3 However, IFRS 12.7(b) requires an entity to disclose information about significant judgements and assumptions it has made in determining that it has significant influence over another entity. [IFRS 12.7(b)]. 2.6 Severe long-term restrictions impairing ability to transfer funds to the investor When assessing its ability to exercise significant influence over an entity, an investor considers the effect of severe long-term restrictions on the transfer of funds from the associate to the investor or other restrictions in exercising significant influence. However, such restrictions do not, in isolation, preclude the exercise of significant influence. [IAS 28.BCZ18]. In some jurisdictions, an entity can seek protection from creditors to reorganise its business (e.g., under # Chapter 11 of the Bankruptcy Code in the United States). In such situations, an investor (that is not under bankruptcy protection itself) with an interest in such an associate will need to evaluate the facts and circumstances to assess whether it is still able to exercise significant influence over the financial and operating policies of the investee. IFRIC Update, November 2016, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Investments in associates and joint ventures Exemptions from applying the equity method Under IAS 28, an entity with joint control of, or significant influence over, an investee accounts for its investment in an associate or a joint venture using the equity method, except when that investment qualifies for exemption in accordance with IAS 28.17 to 19. [IAS 28.16]. These exemptions are discussed in sections 3.1 to 3.4 below. 3.1 Parents exempt from preparing consolidated financial statements An entity need not apply the equity method to its investment in an associate or a joint venture if the entity is a parent that is exempt from preparing consolidated financial statements by the scope exception in IFRS 10.4(a) (see Chapter 7 at section 2.2.1), which exempts subsidiaries from preparing consolidated financial statements if certain criteria are met. [IAS 28.17]. 3.2 Subsidiaries meeting certain criteria Extract from IAS 28 An entity need not apply the equity method to its investment in an associate or a joint venture if the entity is a parent that is exempt from preparing consolidated financial statements by the scope exception in paragraph 4(a) of IFRS 10 or if all the following apply: (a) The entity is a wholly-owned subsidiary, or is a partially-owned subsidiary of another entity and its other owners, including those not otherwise entitled to vote, have been informed about, and do not object to, the entity not applying the equity method. (b) The entity’s debt or equity instruments are not traded in a public market (a domestic or foreign stock exchange or an over-the-counter market, including local and regional markets). (c) The entity did not file, nor is it in the process of filing, its financial statements with a securities commission or other regulatory organisation, for the purpose of issuing any class of instruments in a public market. (d) The ultimate or any intermediate parent of the entity produces financial statements available for public use that comply with IFRSs, in which subsidiaries are consolidated or are measured at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 10. **[IAS 28.17].** An entity is exempt from applying the equity method to its investment in an associate or a joint venture if certain criteria are met. [IAS 28.17]. This exemption applies only when the investor in an associate or a joint venture is not also a parent. If it is a parent, it must look to the similar exemption from preparation of consolidated financial statements in IFRS 10, which also contains the conditions (a) to (d) above for a parent to be exempt from preparing consolidated financial statements under IFRS 10. [IFRS 10.4(a)]. This exemption is available only to entities that are themselves either wholly-owned subsidiaries or partially-owned subsidiaries whose non-controlling shareholders do not object to the presentation of financial statements that do not include associates or joint ventures using the equity method. Subsidiaries are not exempt if none of their parent companies prepares consolidated financial statements in accordance with IFRS accounting standards. A typical example is that of an entity that is a subsidiary of a US group that prepares consolidated financial statements in accordance with US GAAP only. In addition, any entity that has publicly traded debt or equity, or is in the process of obtaining a listing for such instruments, will not satisfy the criteria for exemption. The effect of the above requirements is that a reporting entity that has associates or joint ventures, but no subsidiaries, and does not meet all the criteria in (a)-(d) above, is required to apply equity accounting for its associates or joint ventures in its own (non-consolidated) financial statements (not to be confused with its ‘separate financial statements’ - see section 11 below). Many jurisdictions apply a national GAAP that is based on IFRS but requires some form of endorsement process. The question then arises as to whether the exemption in (d) above can be applied when the ultimate or intermediate parent entity produces financial statements available for public use that comply with a national GAAP that is based on IFRS accounting standards. Investments in associates and joint ventures How we see it In our view, the exemption in (d), which relies on a parent of the entity producing financial statements available for public use that comply with IFRS accounting standards, can be applied when those financial statements use a national GAAP that is based on IFRS accounting standards if certain criteria are met. These are discussed in Chapter 7 at section 2.2.1.D. 3.3 Investments in associates or joint ventures held by venture capital organisations and similar organisations Extract from IAS 28 When an investment in an associate or a joint venture is held by, or is held indirectly through, an entity that is a venture capital organisation, or a mutual fund, unit trust and similar entities including investment-linked insurance funds, the entity may elect to measure that investment at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9. An example of an investment-linked insurance fund is a fund held by an entity as the underlying items for a group of insurance contracts with direct participation features. For the purposes of this election, insurance contracts include investment contracts with discretionary participation features. An entity shall make this election separately for each associate or joint venture, at initial recognition of the associate or joint venture. (See IFRS 17 Insurance Contracts for terms used in this paragraph that are defined in that Standard.) **[IAS 28.18].** This exemption is related to the fact that fair value measurement provides more useful information for users of the financial statements than application of the equity method. In the Basis for Conclusions to IAS 28, the IASB clarified that this is an exemption from the requirement to measure interests in joint ventures and associates using the equity method, rather than an exception to the scope of IAS 28 for the accounting for joint ventures and associates held by these entities. [IAS 28.BC12, IAS 28.BC13]. This exemption raises the question of exactly which entities comprise “venture capital organisations, or a mutual fund, unit trust and similar entities including investment-linked insurance funds”, since they are not defined in IAS 28. The determination of which entities are eligible for this exemption is discussed in sections 3.3.1 and 3.3.2 below. IFRS 17 Insurance Contracts explains that an investment-linked insurance fund could be, for example, a fund held by an entity as the underlying items for a group of insurance contracts with direct participation features. [IAS 28.18]. For the purposes of the fair value election, insurance contracts include investment contracts with discretionary participation features. 3.3.1 Investment entities exception IFRS 10 requires entities that meet the definition of an investment entity to measure investments in subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9. The investment entities exception is discussed further in Chapter 7 at section 10. The application of the investment entity exception is not an accounting policy choice. If an entity meets the definition of an investment entity, it is required to measure its subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss. To meet this definition, an investment entity must, as well as meeting other criteria, elect the exemption from applying the equity method in IAS 28 for its investments in associates and joint ventures. [IFRS 10.B85L(b)]. As discussed further in section 8.2.1 below, if an entity that is not itself an investment entity has an interest in an associate or joint venture that is an investment entity, the investor can retain the fair value measurement applied by that investment entity associate or joint venture to the investment entity associate’s or joint venture’s interests in subsidiaries. [IAS 28.36A]. 3.3.2 Application of IFRS 9 to exempt investments in associates or joint ventures The reason that IAS 28 allows venture capital organisations, mutual funds, unit trusts and similar entities to measure investments in associates and joint ventures at fair value is because these entities often manage their investments based on fair values and so the application of IFRS 9 produces more relevant information. Furthermore, the financial statements would be less useful if changes in the level of ownership in an investment resulted in frequent changes in the method of accounting for the investment. When investments are measured at fair value, the fair value is determined in accordance with IFRS 13 Fair Value Measurement (see Chapter 15). Investments in associates and joint ventures 3.3.2.A Entities with a mixture of activities The exemption clearly applies to venture capital organisations and other similar financial institutions whose main activities consist of managing an investment portfolio comprising investments unrelated to the investor’s business. Although, the exemption is not intended to apply to other entities that hold investments in several associates, there are cases in which entities have significant venture capital activities as well as significant other activities. It is clear from IAS 28.18 that the exemption applies to investments that are indirectly held through a venture capital or similar organisation (as well as to those held by the organisations themselves). Therefore, when an entity has a mixture of activities, if it can demonstrate that it runs a venture capital activity rather than merely undertaking, on an ad hoc basis, transactions that a venture capital activity would undertake, the exemption applies. [IAS 28.18]. Illustration 3-1: Entity owning a discrete venture capital organisation Parent P operates a construction business and owns a venture capital organisation (Subsidiary V) that invests in the telecommunications industry. Even though P itself is not a venture capital organisation, V would be able to apply the exemption and account for its investments at fair value under IFRS 9. In the consolidated financial statements of P, the investments held by V could also be accounted for at fair value under IFRS 9, with changes in fair value recognised in profit or loss in the period of change. Illustration 3-2: Entity with a venture capital organisation segment Bank A has several separate activities. One segment’s business is that of a venture capital organisation. A’s share of investees held by the venture capital organisation segment provides it with significant influence, but not control. Even though A is itself not a venture capital organisation, it would be able to apply the exemption and account for its investments at fair value under IFRS 9, with changes in fair value recognised in profit or loss in the period of change. 3.3.2.B Establishment of a venture capital organisation or similar financial institution IAS 28.18 says that the election to account for an associate or joint venture held by a venture capital or similar organisation is made ‘at initial recognition of the associate or joint venture’. As described at 3.3.2.A above, an entity might conduct a mixture of activities, of which a venture capital or similar organisation is one part. An entity might therefore introduce such an operation and transfer to it existing holdings in associates or joint ventures accounted for using the equity method. This raises a question as to whether the exemption is available on initial transfer of investments to a newly formed venture capital or similar organisation or whether IAS 28.18 is interpreted such that the exemption is available only on initial recognition of the associate or joint venture itself. Arguments for the exemption to be available to relevant investments on transfer to a newly formed venture capital or similar organisation include: • IAS 28 does not provide guidance for when a venture capital or similar organisation is established as part of an internal restructuring. • The election of accounting policy provided by IAS 28.18 becomes available only when a venture capital or similar organisation exists. Prior to this, the entity has made no accounting policy election and so is permitted to do so on initial transfer to the venture capital or similar organisation. • The exemption in IAS 28 is provided because fair value measurement provides more useful information for users of the financial statements than application of the equity method. [IAS 28.BC12]. • If the exemption were not available, associates and joint ventures held by the group on establishment of the venture capital or similar organisation, and transferred to it, would continue to be equity accounted while the exemption would be available for subsequent investments by the venture capital or similar organisation. This might result in mixed measurement models not accurately reflecting the manner in which investments are managed by the venture capital or similar organisation. Investments in associates and joint ventures How we see it In our view, this scenario is not considered in IAS 28. Therefore, it is acceptable to apply the exemption in IAS 28.18 once, on initial transfer of an existing investment in associate or joint venture to a newly established venture capital or similar organisation. It is also acceptable to interpret IAS 28.18 as not permitting such application of the exemption. For the venture capital or similar organisation to have substance, the group must demonstrate that it runs a venture capital activity rather than merely undertaking, on an ad hoc basis, transactions that a venture capital activity would undertake. In the context of a newly established VCO organisation, this might be supported by evidence that the objective of holding the transferred investments in associates or joint ventures is for capital gain. This might be demonstrated by updates and changes to the entity’s: • Group structure • Policies, procedures and business plans • Management structures and remuneration arrangements 3.4 Partial use of fair value measurement of associates Extract from IAS 28 When an entity has an investment in an associate, a portion of which is held indirectly through a venture capital organisation, or a mutual fund, unit trust and similar entities including investment-linked insurance funds, the entity may elect to measure that portion of the investment in the associate at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 regardless of whether the venture capital organisation, or the mutual fund, unit trust and similar entities including investment-linked insurance funds, has significant influence over that portion of the investment. If the entity makes that election, the entity shall apply the equity method to any remaining portion of its investment in an associate that is not held through a venture capital organisation, or a mutual fund, unit trust and similar entities including investment-linked insurance funds. **[IAS 28.19].** In the Basis for Conclusions to IAS 28, the IASB discussed whether the partial use of fair value is allowed only in the case of venture capital organisations (or mutual funds, unit trusts and similar entities including investment-linked insurance funds) that have designated their portion of the investment in the associate at fair value through profit or loss in their own financial statements. The IASB noted that several situations might arise in which those entities do not measure their portion of the investment in the associate at fair value through profit or loss. In those situations, however, from the group’s perspective, the appropriate determination of the business purpose would lead to the measurement of this portion of the investment in the associate at fair value through profit or loss in the consolidated financial statements. Consequently, the *Reference: IASB decided that an entity can measure a portion of an investment in an associate held by a venture* capital organisation (or a mutual fund, unit trust and similar entities including investment-linked insurance funds) at fair value through profit or loss regardless of whether this portion of the investment is measured at fair value through profit or loss in those entities’ financial statements. [IAS 28.BC22]. Illustration 3-3 below (which is based on four scenarios considered by the IFRS IC at its meeting in May 20094) illustrates this partial use exemption. *Reference: IASB Staff Paper 3, Project: IAS 28 Investments in Associates, Paper topic: Venture capital consolidations and partial use of fair* value through profit or loss, IFRIC Meeting, May 2009. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 3-3: Venture capital consolidations and partial use of fair value through profit or loss A parent entity has two wholly-owned subsidiaries (A and B), each of which has an ownership interest in the same associate, Entity C. A is a venture capital business that holds its interest via an investment-linked fund. B is a holding company. Neither of the investments held by A and B is held for trading. Scenario 1: both investments in the associate result in significant influence on a stand-alone basis A accounts for its 25% share in the associate at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 (see # Chapter 46 at section 2.4). B accounts for its 20% share in the associate using the equity method in accordance with IAS 28 (see section 5 below). The parent entity must equity account for its 20% interest held by B. Under the partial use of fair value exemption, the parent entity can elect to measure the 25% interest held by A at fair value through profit or loss. Scenario 2: neither of the investments in the associate results in significant influence on a stand-alone basis but do provide the parent with significant influence on a combined basis. A accounts for its 15% share in the associate at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 (see # Chapter 46 at section 2.4). B designated its 10% share in the associate as at fair value through OCI in accordance with IFRS 9 (see # Chapter 46 at section 2.5). The parent entity must equity account for its 10% interest held by B, even though B would not have significant influence on a stand-alone basis. Under the partial use of fair value exemption, the parent entity can elect to measure the 15% interest held by A at fair value through profit or loss. Scenario 3: one of the investments in the associate results in significant influence on a stand-alone basis and the other investment in the associate does not result in significant influence on a stand-alone basis A accounts for its 25% share in the associate at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 (see # Chapter 46 at section 2.4). B designated its 5% share in the associate as at fair value through OCI in accordance with IFRS 9 (see # Chapter 46 at section 2.5). Investments in associates and joint ventures The parent entity must equity account for its 5% interest held by B, even though B would not have significant influence on a stand-alone basis. Under the partial use of fair value exemption, the parent entity can elect to measure the 25% interest held by A at fair value through profit or loss. Scenario 4: same as scenario 3, but with the ownership interests switched between the subsidiaries A accounts for its 5% share in the associate at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 (see # Chapter 46 at section 2.4). B accounts for its 25% share in the associate using the equity method in accordance with IAS 28 (see section 5 below). The parent entity must equity account for its 25% interest held by B. Under the partial use of fair value exemption, the parent entity can elect to measure the 5% interest held by A at fair value through profit or loss. Investments in associates and joint ventures Classification as held for sale (IFRS 5) IAS 28 requires that an entity applies IFRS 5 Non-current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations to an investment, or a portion of an investment, in an associate or a joint venture that meets the criteria to be classified as held for sale. [IAS 28.20]. The detailed requirements for classification as held for sale are discussed in Chapter 5 at section 2.1.2. In this situation, the investor discontinues the use of the equity method from the date that the investment (or the portion of it) is classified as held for sale. Instead, the associate or joint venture is measured at the lower of its carrying amount and fair value less cost to sell. [IFRS 5.15]. The measurement requirements of IFRS 5 are discussed in Chapter 5 at section 2.2. How we see it In our view, once an investment (or a portion thereof) is classified as held for sale, dividends (or the related portion thereof) are recognised in profit or loss, because the investment (or portion thereof) is no longer being accounted for under the equity method. However, receiving dividends usually reduces the fair value of the investment, which could result in an offsetting loss recognised in the IFRS 5 measurement. Any retained portion of an investment in an associate or a joint venture that has not been classified as held for sale is accounted for using the equity method until disposal of the portion that is classified as held for sale takes place. After the disposal takes place, an entity accounts for any retained interest in the associate or joint venture in accordance with IFRS 9 unless the retained interest continues to be an associate or a joint venture, in which case the entity uses the equity method. [IAS 28.20]. As explained in the Basis for Conclusions to IAS 28, the IASB concluded that if a portion of an interest in an associate or joint venture fulfilled the criteria for classification as held for sale, it is only that portion that is accounted for under IFRS 5. An entity uses the equity method for the retained interest until the portion classified as held for sale is finally sold. This is because, even if the entity has the intention of selling a portion of an interest in an associate or joint venture, until it does so, it still has significant influence over, or joint control of, that investee. [IAS 28.BC26, IAS 28.BC27]. When an investment, or a portion of an investment, in an associate or a joint venture previously classified as held for sale no longer meets the criteria to be so classified, it is accounted for using the equity method retrospectively, from the date of its classification as held for sale. Financial statements for the periods since classification as held for sale are amended accordingly. [IAS 28.21]. Investments in associates and joint ventures Overview of the equity method IAS 28 defines the equity method as “a method of accounting whereby the investment is initially recognised at cost and adjusted thereafter for the post-acquisition change in the investor’s share of the investee’s net assets. The investor’s profit or loss includes its share of the investee’s profit or loss and the investor’s other comprehensive income includes its share of the investee’s other comprehensive income.” [IAS 28.3]. Extract from IAS 28 Under the equity method, on initial recognition the investment in an associate or a joint venture is recognised at cost, and the carrying amount is increased or decreased to recognise the investor’s share of the profit or loss of the investee after the date of acquisition. The investor’s share of the investee’s profit or loss is recognised in the investor’s profit or loss. Distributions received from an investee reduce the carrying amount of the investment. Adjustments to the carrying amount may also be necessary for changes in the investor’s proportionate interest in the investee arising from changes in the investee’s other comprehensive income. Such changes include those arising from the revaluation of property, plant and equipment and from foreign exchange translation differences. The investor’s share of those changes is recognised in the investor’s other comprehensive income (see IAS 1 Presentation of Financial Statements). An investment is accounted for using the equity method from the date on which it becomes an associate or a joint venture. On acquisition of the investment, any difference between the cost of the investment and the entity’s share of the net fair value of the investee’s identifiable assets and liabilities is accounted for as follows: (a) Goodwill relating to an associate or a joint venture is included in the carrying amount of the investment. Amortisation of that goodwill is not permitted. (b) Any excess of the entity’s share of the net fair value of the investee’s identifiable assets and liabilities over the cost of the investment is included as income in the determination of the entity’s share of the associate or joint venture’s profit or loss in the period in which the investment is acquired. Appropriate adjustments to the entity’s share of the associate’s or joint venture’s profit or loss after acquisition are made in order to account, for example, for depreciation of the depreciable assets based on their fair values at the acquisition date. Similarly, appropriate adjustments to the entity’s share of the associate’s or joint venture’s profit or loss after acquisition are made for impairment losses such as for goodwill or property, plant and equipment. **[IAS 28.10, IAS 28.32].** IAS 28 explains that equity accounting is necessary because recognising income simply based on distributions received might not be an adequate measure of the income earned by an investor on an investment in an associate or a joint venture, since distributions received might bear little relation to the performance of the associate or joint venture. Through its significant influence over the associate, or joint control of the joint venture, the investor has an interest in the associate’s or joint venture’s performance and, as a result, the return on its investment. The investor accounts for this interest by extending the scope of its financial statements to include its share of profits or losses of such an investee. As a result, application of the equity method provides more informative reporting of the net assets and profit or loss of the investor. [IAS 28.11]. The investor’s profit or loss and other comprehensive income are also adjusted for differences between the carrying amount of assets and liabilities in the associate’s or joint venture’s books and that in the investor’s books, for example resulting from fair value adjustments on acquisition of the associate or joint venture. These are sometimes referred to as basis differences. [IAS 28.32]. Basis differences are important, because when an investor records its equity method earnings, the entity reflects not only its portion of earnings of the associate or joint venture, but also any effects of the basis differences related to identified assets or assumed liabilities, for example, by recognising additional depreciation on a property that had a fair value greater than its carrying amount in the associate’s or joint venture’s books at the date of acquisition. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 5-1: Application of the equity method On the first day of its financial year, Entity A acquires a 35% interest in Entity B, over which it can exercise significant influence. A paid CU475,000 for its interest in B. The book value of B’s net identifiable assets at acquisition date was CU900,000, and the fair value CU1.1m. The difference of CU200,000 relates to an item of property, plant and equipment (PP&E) with a remaining useful life of 10 years. During the year, B made a profit of CU80,000 and paid a dividend of CU120,000. B also owned an investment in securities classified as at fair value through OCI that increased in value by CU20,000 during the year. For the purposes of the example, any deferred tax implications have been ignored. A accounts for its investment in B under the equity method as follows: CU CU Acquisition date of investment in B Share in book value of B’s net identifiable assets: 35% of **CU900,000** 315,000 Share in fair valuation of B’s net identifiable assets: 35% of (CU1.1m - CU900,000)* 70,000 Goodwill on investment in B: CU475,000 - CU315,000 - CU70,000* 90,000 Cost of investment 475,000 Profit during the year Share in the profit reported by B: 35% of CU80,000 28,000 Adjustment to reflect effect of fair valuation* 35% of ((CU1.1m - CU900,000) ÷ 10 years) (7,000) Share of profit in B recognised in income by A (equity method income) 21,000 Revaluation of asset at fair value through OCI Share in revaluation recognised in OCI by A: 35% of CU20,000 7,000 Dividend received by A during the year 35% of CU120,000 (debit cash, credit investment) (42,000) End of the financial year Share in book value of B’s net assets: **CU315,000 + 35% (CU80,000 - CU120,000 + CU20,000)** 308,000 Share in fair valuation of B’s net identifiable assets: CU70,000 - **CU7,000*** 63,000 Goodwill on investment in B* 90,000 Closing balance of A’s investment in B 461,000 * These line items are often referred to as ‘basis differences’ and are combined with the ones immediately above. 5.1 Comparison between equity accounting and consolidation For some time, there has been a debate about whether the equity method of accounting is primarily a method of consolidation or a method of valuing an investment, as IAS 28 does not provide specific guidance either way. An investor that controls a subsidiary has control over the assets and liabilities of that subsidiary. While an investor that has significant influence over an associate or joint control of a joint venture controls its holding in the shares of the associate or joint venture, it does not control the assets and liabilities of that associate or joint venture. Therefore, the investor does not account for the assets and the liabilities of the associate or joint venture, but accounts only for its investment in the associate or joint venture as a whole. Investments in associates and joint ventures Extract from IAS 28 Many of the procedures that are appropriate for the application of the equity method are similar to the consolidation procedures described in IFRS 10. Furthermore, the concepts underlying the procedures used in accounting for the acquisition of a subsidiary are also adopted in accounting for the acquisition of an investment in an associate or a joint venture. **[IAS 28.26].** However, although the equity method generally adopts consolidation principles (see Chapter 8), it also has features of a valuation methodology. For example, the IASB noted that IAS 28.20 (now IAS 28.26) explains “only the methodology used to account for investments in associates. This should not be taken to imply that the principles for business combinations and consolidations can be applied by analogy to accounting for investments in associates and joint ventures.” [IFRS 9.BCZ2.42]. The similarities between equity accounting and consolidation include: • Appropriate adjustments to the entity’s share of the associate’s or joint venture’s profits or losses after acquisition are made to account, for example, for depreciation of the depreciable assets based on their fair values at the acquisition date. • Goodwill relating to an associate or a joint venture is recognised in the carrying amount of the investment. • Goodwill is not amortised. • Any excess of the investor’s share of the net fair value of the associate’s identifiable assets and liabilities over the cost of the investment is included as income in the determination of the entity’s share of the associate’s or joint venture’s profit or loss in the period in which the investment is acquired (that is, any excess is recognised as a bargain purchase gain). • Unrealised profits on ‘upstream’ and ‘downstream’ transactions are eliminated, although, unlike consolidation, only until realised in transactions with third parties (see section 7.1 below). • Uniform accounting policies are applied for like transactions. However, there are also several differences between equity accounting and consolidation, including: • The investor ceases to recognise its share of losses of an associate or joint venture once the investment has been reduced to zero. • The treatment of loans and borrowings (including preference shares classified as debt by the investee) between the reporting entity and its associates or joint ventures (see section 7.3 below). • The investor cannot capitalise its own borrowing costs in respect of an associate’s or joint venture’s assets under construction (an equity accounted investment is not a qualifying asset under IAS 23 Borrowing Costs regardless of the associate’s or joint venture’s activities or assets). [IAS 23.BC22]. • The investor considers whether there is any additional impairment loss with respect to its net investment. As there is no clear principle underlying the application of the equity method, different views on how to account for certain transactions for which the standard has no clear guidance might be taken, depending on which principle (i.e., consolidation or valuation of an investment) is deemed to take precedence. We address these issues in the following sections. Investments in associates and joint ventures Initial accounting for an associate or joint venture 6.1 Date of commencement of equity accounting An investor begins equity accounting for an associate or a joint venture from the date on which it obtains significant influence over the associate or joint control over the joint venture (and is not otherwise exempt from equity accounting for it). [IAS 28.32]. In most situations, this is when the investor acquires the investment in the associate or joint venture. Determining whether an entity has significant influence is discussed in section 2 above. 6.2 Initial carrying amount of an associate or joint venture Under the equity method, an investment is initially recognised at cost. [IAS 28.3, IAS 28.10]. However, ‘cost’ for this purpose is not defined. Frequently asked questions Question 6-1: Is directly attributable expenditure included in the initial carrying amount of an investment under the equity method? In July 2009, the IFRS IC discussed the lack of a definition of cost in IAS 28. The IFRS IC issued an agenda decision5 clarifying that the cost of an investment in an associate at initial recognition comprises its purchase price and any directly attributable expenditures necessary to obtain it. Therefore, any acquisition-related costs are not expensed (as is the case in a business combination under IFRS 3 Business Combinations) but are included as part of the cost of the associate. The glossary to IFRS accounting standards defines cost as being the “amount of cash or cash equivalents paid or the fair value of the other consideration given to acquire an asset at the time of its acquisition or construction …”. ‘Consideration given’ is likewise not defined, but guidance exists in IFRS accounting standards: • ‘Consideration transferred’ in the context of a business combination, as referred to in IFRS 3.37. **[IFRS 3.37].** • ‘Cost’ as applied in relation to acquisitions of PP&E in accordance with IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment, intangible assets in accordance with IAS 38 Intangible Assets and investment property in accordance with IAS 40 Investment Property. Applying the requirements of IFRS 3 Business Combinations, the ‘consideration transferred’ in a business combination comprises the sum of the acquisition-date fair values of assets transferred by the acquirer, liabilities incurred by the acquirer to the former owners of the acquiree, and equity interests issued by the acquirer. This includes any liability (or asset) for contingent consideration, which is measured and recognised at fair value at the acquisition date. Subsequent changes in the measurement of the liability (or asset) are recognised in profit or loss (see Chapter 10 at section 7.1). How we see it In our view, the guidance in IFRS 3 referred to above can be applied to contingent consideration arrangements for the purchase of an associate or a joint venture, i.e., the initial carrying amount of an associate or joint venture includes the fair value of any contingent consideration arrangement. Consequently, subsequent changes in the contingent consideration would be accounted for under IFRS 9. Further guidance on accounting for contingent consideration when determining the cost of an asset can be found in Chapter 18 at section 4.5 and Chapter 19 at section 4.1.9. The considerations regarding applying the cost requirements of other standards have previously been discussed by the IFRS IC, and the discussions are summarised in Chapter 9 at section 2.1.1. The IFRS IC agenda decision did not provide any specific guidance in relation to a step acquisition of an associate or a joint venture. This is discussed further in section 6.2.3 below. Agenda decision, July 2009. Potential effect of IFRS 3 Business Combinations (as revised in 2008) and IAS 27 Consolidated and Separate Financial Statements (as amended in 2008) on equity method accounting. Investments in associates and joint ventures 6.2.1 Measurement period on acquisition of an associate or joint venture IAS 28 does not provide guidance when the accounting as required by IAS 28.32 is incomplete by the end of the reporting period in which the acquisition of the investment occurs. How we see it We believe that when the initial accounting for the acquisition of an associate or joint venture is incomplete at the reporting date, an entity could use by analogy the guidance in IFRS 3 regarding provisional accounting in the measurement period (see Chapter 10 at section 12). Hence, the entity estimates and discloses provisional amounts for the items for which the accounting is incomplete. Those provisional amounts are adjusted retrospectively in the following reporting period to reflect new information obtained about facts and circumstances that existed at the acquisition date that, if known, would have affected the amounts recognised at that date. This is because the concepts underlying the procedures used in accounting for the acquisition of a subsidiary are also adopted in accounting for the acquisition of an investment in an associate or a joint venture. [IAS 28.26]. 6.2.2 Initial carrying amount of an associate or joint venture following loss of control of an entity A parent might lose control of its subsidiary as a result of a transaction with a third party or a downstream transaction (i.e., a transaction with its associate or joint venture, see section 7.5 below). If a parent loses control of a subsidiary that constitutes a business in a transaction that is not a downstream transaction and the retained interest is an investment in an associate or joint venture, then it applies **IFRS 10.25.** Under IFRS 10, when an entity loses control of a subsidiary and retains an interest in an associate or joint venture, the retained interest is remeasured at its fair value and included in the calculation of the gain or loss on disposal of the subsidiary. This fair value becomes the cost on initial recognition of the associate or joint venture. [IFRS 10.25]. The gain or loss on disposal is recognised in full and not restricted as would be the case in certain downstream transactions as explained below. Under IAS 28, the investor that sells or contributes assets to its equity-accounted associate or joint venture is required to restrict any gain arising on its own assets to the gain attributable to the other party to the associate or joint venture. [IAS 28.28]. This results in an adjustment to the carrying amount of the investment in the associate or joint venture. In certain scenarios in which a parent loses control of a subsidiary in a downstream transaction (see examples in Figure 6-1 below), there is a conflict between the above requirements of IFRS 10 and IAS 28. In September 2014, the IASB issued Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture (amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28) to address the conflict between IFRS 10 and IAS 28.6 The amendments state that: • Partial gain or loss recognition for transactions between an investor and its associate or joint venture applies only to a gain or loss resulting from the sale or contribution of assets that do not constitute a business as defined in IFRS 3. • The gain or loss resulting from the sale or contribution of assets that constitute a business as defined in IFRS 3 to an associate or joint venture is recognised in full. [IAS 28.31A]. In addition, the amendments address when an entity sells or contributes assets in two or more transactions. When determining whether assets that are sold or contributed constitute a business as defined in IFRS 3, an entity considers whether the sale or contribution of those assets is part of multiple arrangements that are accounted for as a single transaction in accordance with the requirements in IFRS 10.B97. [IAS 28.31B]. In December 2015, the IASB deferred the effective date of these amendments indefinitely due to feedback that the recognition of a partial gain or loss when a transaction involves assets that do not constitute a business, even if these assets are housed in a subsidiary, is inconsistent with the initial measurement requirements of IAS 28.32(b): “Any excess of the entity’s share of the net fair value of the investee’s identifiable assets and liabilities over the cost of the investment is included as income in the determination of the entity’s share of the associate or joint venture’s profit or loss in the period in which the investment is acquired”. This issue is in the scope of the equity method project (see section 13 below). However, entities can apply the amendments before the effective date. Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture (amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28), IASB, September 2014. Investments in associates and joint ventures How we see it Until the amendments to IAS 28 become mandatorily effective, we believe that entities have an accounting policy choice to apply either IFRS 10 or IAS 28 to the contribution of an interest in a subsidiary that constitutes a business. Once selected, the entity must apply the policy consistently. 6.2.2.A Initial accounting for the investment in an associate or joint venture using the IFRS 10 approach and the IAS 28 approach When the IFRS 10 approach is applied to a loss of control transaction, an investor accounts for a retained equity method investment as if it had acquired the retained investment at fair value at the date that control is lost. [IFRS 10.25, IAS 28.32]. Therefore, the accounting is the same as for the initial acquisition of an investment in an associate according to IAS 28.32 (see section 5 above). The investor applying the equity method also needs to consider the requirements in IAS 28.32 regarding initial investment in an associate or joint venture. On initial recognition, the concepts underlying the procedures used in accounting for the acquisition of a subsidiary are also adopted in accounting for the acquisition of an investment in an associate or joint venture and fair values are applied to measure all the identifiable assets and liabilities in calculating any goodwill or bargain purchase that exists. [IAS 28.26, IAS 28.32]. Accordingly, to apply the equity method from the date control is lost, an investor remeasures all the identifiable assets and liabilities underlying the investment at their fair values (or other measurement basis required by IFRS 3 at that date), i.e., the investor performs a new purchase price allocation. Illustration 6-1 below demonstrates the accounting under the IFRS 10 approach. Although it shows the loss of control of a business with a retained interest in an associate, the accounting would be the same if the retained interest were a joint venture. Illustration 6-1: Accounting for retained interest in an associate following loss of control of a subsidiary that is a business due to sale of shares to third party Entity A owns 100% of the shares of Entity B. B meets the definition of a business. On its reporting date, A sells 60% of the shares in B to Entity C for CU1.3m. As a result of the sale, A loses control over B. A retains a 40% equity interest in B and determines that it has significant influence over B. At the date of disposal, the carrying amount of the net identifiable assets of B in A’s consolidated financial statements is CU1.2m and the fair value of A’s retained interest of 40% of the shares of B is CU800,000. Upon A’s sale of 60% of the shares of B, it deconsolidates B and accounts for its investment in B as an associate using the equity method of accounting, as follows: Dr Cash **CU1,300,000** Dr Associate **CU800,000** Cr Net assets of subsidiary **CU1,200,000** Cr Gain on disposal **CU900,000** When the IAS 28 approach is applied to a loss of control transaction, the retained equity method investment is adjusted to restrict any gain arising on the investor’s own assets to the gain attributable to the other party to the associate or joint venture. Therefore, the investment is not initially measured at its fair value. For an illustration of the IAS 28 approach, see section 7.5.2 below. 6.2.2.B Loss of control scenarios in which the retained investment is an associate or joint venture As discussed in more detail in Chapter 8 at section 3.3.2.A, examples of situations that result in the loss of control of a subsidiary and the interest retained is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method, include: • Scenario 1 - a ‘downstream’ sale or contribution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is a business) to an existing associate or joint venture. • Scenario 2 - a ‘downstream’ sale or contribution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is not a business) to an existing associate or joint venture. • Scenario 3 - a direct sale or dilution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is a business) for cash in a transaction involving a third party with a retained interest in an associate or joint venture. • Scenario 4 - a direct sale or dilution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is not a business) for cash in a transaction involving a third party with a retained interest in an associate or joint venture. Investments in associates and joint ventures The contribution of non-monetary assets to an associate or joint venture, which could include the contribution of a subsidiary, is discussed at 7.5 below. In certain of these scenarios noted below there is a conflict between the requirements of IFRS 10.25 and those of IAS 28.28. This conflict is described at section 6.2.2 above. How we see it In our view, the context of which is discussed more fully in Chapter 8 at 3.3.2.A, the following accounting policy determinations are needed: (a) Do the requirements of IFRS 10.25 apply to: • A loss of control of a subsidiary that is a business (‘narrow view’) Or • A loss of control of a subsidiary (whether a business or not) (‘wide view’)? (b) Where there is a conflict between the requirements of IFRS 10.25 (‘IFRS 10 approach’) and IAS 28.28 (‘IAS 28 approach’), which accounting standard takes precedence? (c) Where the transaction falls within the scope of neither IFRS 10.25 nor IAS 28.28, what accounting policy is applied? Scenarios 1 and 3 fall within the scope of IFRS 10.25, whether a ‘wide view’ or ‘narrow view’ is taken at (a) above. However, Scenarios 2 and 4 will fall within the scope of IFRS 10 only if a ‘wide view’ is taken. Only Scenarios 1 and 2 fall within the scope of IAS 28.28. This is because this requirement in IAS 28 applies only to ‘downstream’ transactions with an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method and not to all transactions where the retained interest in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method. Figure 6-1 below summarises how the above policy determinations apply to the four scenarios where the September 2014 amendments are not applied. Application of the September 2014 amendments is discussed at 6.2.2 above. Figure 6-1: Loss of control transactions where the retained interest in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method Subsidiary meets the definition of a business Subsidiary does not meet the definition of a business Sale or contribution of investment in former subsidiary in downstream transaction with existing associate or joint venture Scenario 1 • Both IFRS 10.25 (as the subsidiary is a business) and IAS 28.28 (as this is a ‘downstream’ transaction) apply to this scenario, so a conflict arises. • An entity would need to develop an accounting policy to resolve the conflict, which would result in either applying full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) or partial gain recognition (IAS 28 approach). Scenario 2 • IAS 28.28 (as this is a ‘downstream’ transaction) applies to this scenario. Therefore, partial gain recognition applies. • However, if a ‘wide view’ is taken on the scope of IFRS 10.25, a conflict arises. An entity would then need to develop an accounting policy to resolve the conflict, which would result in either applying full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) or partial gain recognition (IAS 28 approach). Sale or dilution of investment in former subsidiary for cash in transaction with third party Scenario 3 • IAS 28.28 does not apply (as this is not a ‘downstream’ transaction). • IFRS 10.25 applies (as the subsidiary is a business). • Therefore, an entity applies full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) to the transaction. Scenario 4 • IAS 28.28 does not apply (as this is not a ‘downstream transaction’). • IFRS 10.25 only applies if a ‘wide view’ is taken. • Therefore, if a ‘wide view’ over the scope of IFRS 10.25 applies, an entity applies full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) to the transaction. • If a ‘narrow view’ over the scope of IFRS 10.25 applies, an entity must develop and apply an appropriate accounting policy that provides relevant and reliable information in accordance with IAS 8. Investments in associates and joint ventures How we see it Where it is determined that a loss of control transaction falls within the scope of neither IFRS 10.25 nor IAS 28.28 (which might be the case for Scenario 4 if a ‘narrow view’ of the scope of IFRS 10.25 is taken), management will need to use judgement in developing and applying an appropriate accounting policy that results in relevant and reliable information, which must be consistently applied. Please see further discussion of matters relating to developing an appropriate accounting policy in Chapter 8 at section 3.3.2.A. 6.2.3 Step acquisition of an associate or joint venture (financial instrument becoming an associate or joint venture) An entity might gain significant influence or joint control over an existing investment upon acquisition of a further interest or due to a change in circumstances. IAS 28 is unclear on how an investor accounts for an existing investment that is accounted for under IFRS 9 and that subsequently becomes an associate or a joint venture, to be accounted for under the equity method. How we see it In our view, when accounting for the step acquisition of an associate or a joint venture, an investor applies either: (a) An ‘accumulated cost’ approach Or (b) A ‘fair value as deemed cost’ approach Once selected, the investor must apply the selected policy consistently. The rationale for each of these approaches is discussed in more detail below. As discussed in section 6.2 above, the IFRS IC, in July 2009, clarified that the initial cost of an equity accounted investment comprises the purchase price and directly attributable expenditures necessary to obtain it. Although the implications for the step acquisition of an associate are not explicitly addressed, it appeared that, since the IFRS IC considered that the initial recognition of the associate is based on its cost, the accounting reflects an ‘accumulated cost’ approach. However, in July 2010, the IFRS IC received a request to address the accounting for an investment in an associate when the investment was purchased in stages and classified as available-for-sale (AFS) in terms of IAS 39 Financial Instruments: Recognition and Measurement until it became an associate.7 The Staff Paper recommended that “the fair value of an investment classified as AFS prior to the investor obtaining significant influence over that investment should be the deemed cost of that pre-existing interest at the date the investor obtains significant influence over the associate. The accumulated changes in fair value accounted for in OCI should be reclassified to profit or loss at that date.” The Staff Paper further recommended that a clarification of IAS 28 to that effect be included within the Annual Improvements project.8 Thus, the Staff was recommending a ‘fair value as deemed cost’ approach. However, due to the acknowledged diversity in practice in accounting for associates purchased in stages, the IFRS IC did not reach a conclusion and to date there has been no amendment to IAS 28.9 In January 2019, the IFRS IC issued an agenda decision regarding the step acquisition of an investment in a subsidiary accounted for at cost in the separate financial statements of the parent. In the request, the initial interest was an equity instrument of another entity and was measured at fair value in accordance with IFRS 9. The IFRS IC concluded that a reasonable reading of the requirements in IFRS accounting standards could result in the application of either one of the two approaches to determine ‘cost’: the ‘fair value as deemed cost’ approach or the ‘accumulated cost’ approach. These approaches are discussed in more detail below. The IFRS IC concluded that, when the accumulated cost approach is applied, any difference between the fair value of the initial interest at the date of obtaining control of the subsidiary and its original consideration meets the definitions of income or expenses in the Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting. Accordingly, applying paragraph 88 of IAS 1 Presentation of Financial Statements, the entity recognises this difference in profit or loss, regardless of whether, before obtaining control, the entity had presented subsequent changes in fair value of the initial interest in profit or loss or in OCI.10 IFRIC Update, July 2010, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. *Reference: IASB Staff Paper 16, Project: Annual Improvements Project - 2009-2011 cycle, Paper topic: IAS 28 - Investments in Associates -* Purchase in stages - fair value as deemed cost, IFRS IC Meeting, July 2010, paras. 24 and 29. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, July 2010, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, January 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Investments in associates and joint ventures How we see it The January 2019 agenda decision related specifically to investments in subsidiaries in the separate financial statements of the parent. However, we believe it is also relevant to step acquisitions of equity accounted associates and joint ventures, because IAS 28.3 also requires investments in associates and joint ventures to be measured at cost. The scope of the equity method project (see section 13 below), includes measuring the cost of an investment when an investor obtains significant influence and has a previously held interest in the investee. Readers should monitor developments.11 6.2.3.A Applying an accumulated cost approach When an accumulated cost approach is applied to account for a step acquisition of an associate or a joint venture, this involves the determination of: (a) The cost of the investment (b) Whether or not any catch-up adjustment is required when first applying equity accounting (i.e., an adjustment for the share of investee’s profits and other equity movements as if the previously held interest had been equity accounted) (c) The date(s) at which the goodwill implicit in the investment (or gain on bargain purchase) is measured Not all of these aspects of the accounting for a step acquisition of an associate or a joint venture were addressed by the IFRS IC agenda decisions in 2009 and 2019. How we see it In our view, there are four possible accumulated cost approaches that can be applied to a step acquisition of an associate or a joint venture. Once selected, the investor must apply the selected policy consistently. In all accumulated cost approaches, cost is the sum of the consideration given for each tranche together with any directly attributable costs. However, because of the answers to (b) and (c) above, the four accumulated cost approaches are as follows: Catch-up equity accounting adjustment for previously held interest Determination of goodwill/gain on bargain purchase Approach 1 None Difference between sum of the consideration and share of fair value of net identifiable assets at date investment becomes an associate or joint venture Approach 2 None Difference between the cost of each tranche and the share of fair value of net identifiable assets acquired in each tranche Approach 3 For profits (less dividends), and changes in OCI Approach 4 For profits (less dividends), changes in OCI and unrecognised changes in fair value of net identifiable assets The basis for using the above accumulated cost approaches can be set out as follows.
