The explanations below assume that the step acquisition has occurred in two tranches; the second acquisition resulting in the entity obtaining significant influence. When an entity purchases multiple tranches prior to obtaining significant influence, references below to the ‘first tranche’ apply to all tranches prior to obtaining significant influence. *Reference: IASB Update, April 2022, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Investments in associates and joint ventures Approach 1 IAS 28.32 (see section 5 above) states that an investment is accounted for using the equity method “from the date on which it becomes an associate or a joint venture.” Recognising any catch-up adjustments can be interpreted as a form of equity accounting for a period prior to gaining significant influence, which contradicts this principle of IAS 28. IAS 28.32 also states that any notional goodwill or gain on a bargain purchase is determined “on acquisition of the investment”. However, it does not specify on which date the fair values of the net identifiable assets are determined. It can be interpreted to mean only at the date that the investment becomes an associate or a joint venture. This is also consistent with the approach in IFRS 3, whereby the underlying fair values of net identifiable assets are determined at one time only, rather than determining them several times for individual transactions leading to the change in the economic event. Approach 2 No catch-up adjustment is recognised, for the same reasons as noted in Approach 1. IAS 28.32 is interpreted to mean that the fair values of the associate’s or joint venture’s net identifiable assets are determined at a date that corresponds to the date at which consideration was given. Therefore, the fair values are determined for each tranche. This might require the fair values to be determined for previous periods when no such exercise was performed at the date of the original purchase. Approach 3 A catch-up adjustment is recognised to reflect the application of the equity method as described in IAS 28.10 (see section 5 above), with respect to the first tranche (i.e., the share of the associate’s results that would have been recognised by the investor if the first tranche of 10% had always been equity accounted). However, the application of that paragraph restricts the adjustment to only the share of profits (less dividends) and OCI relating to the first tranche. That is, there is no catch-up adjustment made for changes in the fair value of the net identifiable assets not recognised by the investee (except for any adjustments necessary to give effect to uniform accounting policies). The determination of goodwill is the same as for Approach 2. Approach 4 This approach is based on the underlying philosophy of equity accounting, which is to reflect the investor’s share of the underlying net identifiable assets plus goodwill inherent in the purchase price. Therefore, when the investment was acquired in tranches, IAS 28.32 is interpreted to mean that a catch-up adjustment in respect of the first tranche is necessary, in order to apply equity accounting from the date the investment becomes an associate or a joint venture. The catch-up adjustment reflects not only the post-acquisition share of profits and OCI relating to the first tranche (i.e., the share of the associate’s or joint venture’s results that would have been recognised by the investor if the first tranche of 10% had always been equity accounted), but also the share of the unrecognised fair value adjustments based on the fair values of the net identifiable assets at the date of becoming an associate or a joint venture. The determination of goodwill is the same as for Approach 2. Example of cost accumulation approaches The above four accumulated cost approaches are shown in Illustration 6-2 below. Although, the example illustrates the step acquisition of an associate, the accounting would be the same if the transaction had resulted in the step acquisition of a joint venture. Illustration 6-2: Accounting for existing financial instruments on the step acquisition of an associate (cost-based approach) An investor acquired a 10% interest in an investee for CU100 on 1 January 20X1, which increased to a fair value of CU150 as of 31 December 20X3. Until 31 December 20X3, the investor accounted for its initial 10% interest at fair value in accordance with IFRS 9 and recognised the difference between the fair value of CU150 and the cost of the first tranche of CU100 of CU50 and the dividends received for its 10% interest of CU20 (CU200 * 10%) in profit or loss. Three years later, on 1 January 20X4, the investor acquired an additional 15% interest in the investee for CU225. The investor now holds a 25% interest and has the ability to exercise significant influence. For the purposes of the example, directly attributable transaction costs and any deferred tax implications have been ignored. Investments in associates and joint ventures The financial information relating to the investee can be summarised as follows: ## January 20X1 Movements in period of 10% ownership January 20X4 Profit Dividends Increase in fair value of land Increase in fair value of goodwill CU CU CU CU CU CU Book value of investee’s assets (200) Investor’s fair value adjustment to net identifiable assets (land*) Investor’s goodwill Fair value of investee 1,000 1,500 Consideration paid * For simplicity, the example includes fair value adjustments only on a non-depreciable asset. The accounting for this step acquisition under each of the accumulated cost approaches is as follows (all amounts in CU): Catch-up equity accounting adjustment Initial equity accounted amount Goodwill in initial amount of associate Approach 1 100 + 225 = 325 Consideration less share of fair value of net identifiable assets at time investment becomes an associate 325 - (25% × 1200) = 25 Approach 2 100 + 225 = 325 Cost less share of fair value of net identifiable assets at each tranche: 100 - (10% × 800) = 20 + 225 - (15% × 1200) = 45 Total = 65 Approach 3 10% of profits (less dividends) 10% × (500 - 200) = 30 325 + 30 = 355 Approach 4 10% of profits (less dividends) and changes in unrecognised fair value of net identifiable assets 10% × (500 - 200 + 100) = 325 + 40 = 365 The result in both the separate and consolidated financial statements is as follows: Cost of investment In all four accumulated cost approaches, the difference of CU50, between the fair value of the original 10% of CU150 and the cost of the first tranche of CU100, is reversed against profit or loss on 1 January 20X4 (the date of the transaction) to bring the asset back to its original cost of CU100. Catch-up adjustment For the accumulated cost approaches 3 and 4, the ‘catch-up’ equity accounting adjustment relating to the first tranche is recognised against the appropriate balance within equity - that is, retained earnings, or other equity reserve, depending on the nature of the ‘catch up’. For example, an adjustment relating to the investee’s profits would be recognised against retained earnings and an adjustment relating to the investee’s OCI, for example, foreign currency translation, would be recognised against the relevant equity reserve. Investments in associates and joint ventures The combined journal entry at 1 January 20X4 under each of the four approaches is as follows: Approach 1 Approach 2 Approach 3 Approach 4 Associate Dr 325 (of which equity method goodwill is 25) Dr 325 (of which equity method goodwill is 65) Dr 355 (of which equity method goodwill is 65) Dr 365 (of which equity method goodwill is 65) Cash (Credit) Cr 225 Cr 225 Cr 225 Cr 225 Investment **(IFRS 9)** (Credit) Cr 150 Cr 150 Cr 150 Cr 150 Profit or loss Dr 50 (reversing prior fair value increases) Dr 50 (reversing prior fair value increases) Dr 50 (reversing prior fair value increases) Dr 50 (reversing prior fair value increases) Retained earnings or other equity reserve No change to prior equity method income or dividend income No change to prior equity method income or dividend income Cr 50 (cumulative equity method income) Dr 20 (reversing prior dividend income) Cr 50 (cumulative equity method income) Cr 10 (unrecognised increase in fair value of net identifiable assets) Dr 20 (reversing prior dividend income) 6.2.3.B Applying a fair value as deemed cost approach When a fair value as deemed cost approach is applied to accounting for a step acquisition of an associate or a joint venture, the fair value of the previously held interest at the date that significant influence or joint control is obtained is deemed to be the cost for the initial application of equity accounting. Since the investment would already be measured at fair value under IFRS 9, there is no further change to its carrying value. • If the investment had been accounted for at fair value through OCI under IFRS 9, amounts accumulated in equity are not reclassified to profit or loss at the date that significant influence is obtained, although the cumulative gain or loss can be transferred within equity. [IFRS 9.5.2.1, IFRS 9.5.7.5, IFRS 9.B5.7.1, **IFRS 9.B5.7.3].** • If the investment had been accounted for at fair value through profit or loss under IFRS 9, any changes from original cost would already be reflected in profit or loss. Consistent with the guidance in IFRS 3 for acquisitions achieved in stages, the calculation of goodwill at the date the investor obtains significant influence or joint control is made only at that date, using information available at that date. The fair value as deemed cost approach is shown in Illustration 6-3 below. Although the example illustrates the step acquisition of an associate, the accounting would be the same if the transaction had resulted in the step acquisition of a joint venture. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 6-3: Accounting for existing financial instruments on the step acquisition of an associate (fair value (IFRS 3) approach) Using the same information as in Illustration 6-2 above, under a fair value as deemed cost approach to an acquisition in stages, in the consolidated financial statements of the investor, the fair value of the 10% existing interest would be deemed to be part of the cost for the initial application of equity accounting. Equity method goodwill is calculated as the difference between the initial equity method investment of CU375 (the fair value of the existing 10% interest of CU150 and the cost of the additional 15% interest of CU225) and CU300 (25% of the fair value of net identifiable assets of CU1,200, at the date significant influence is attained). The combined journal entry at 1 January 20X4 is as follows: Dr Associate CU375 Cr Investment CU150 Cr Cash CU225 Additionally, if the investor measured the original investment at fair value through OCI, any amount in OCI relating to this interest can be reclassified within equity. The methodology shown in Illustration 6-3 above is consistent with the accounting that is required by IAS 28 in the reverse situation; i.e., when there is a loss of significant influence in an associate (or loss of joint control in a joint venture), resulting in the discontinuance of the equity method (see section 9 below). 6.2.4 Step increase in an existing associate or joint venture without a change in status of the investee An entity might acquire an additional interest in an existing associate that continues to be an associate accounted for under the equity method. Similarly, an entity might acquire an additional interest in an existing joint venture that continues to be a joint venture accounted for under the equity method. IAS 28 does not explicitly deal with such transactions. How we see it When an investor acquires an additional interest in an existing associate that continues to be accounted for under the equity method, we believe that the purchase price paid for the additional interest is added to the existing carrying amount of the associate or the joint venture and the existing interest in the associate or joint venture is not remeasured. This increase in the investment must still be split notionally between goodwill and the additional interest in the fair value of the net identifiable assets of the associate or joint venture. This split is based on the fair value of the net identifiable assets at the date of the increase in the associate or joint venture. However, no remeasurement is made for previously unrecognised changes in the fair values of net identifiable assets. The treatment is shown in Illustration 6-4 below. This accounting differs from the treatment required by IFRS 3 when an increased investment in an associate or joint venture results in the investor obtaining control over the investee. IFRS 3 is clear that when an entity acquires an additional interest in an existing associate or joint venture that gives it control, the entity is required to revalue the previously held interest in that investment and any gain or loss is recognised in profit or loss. [IFRS 3.41, IFRS 3.42]. This treatment reflects the significant change in the nature of, and economic circumstances surrounding, that investment, which therefore warrants a change in the classification and measurement of that investment. [IFRS 3.BC384]. By contrast, when an investor increases its ownership interest in an existing associate or joint venture and that investment’s classification remains unchanged, there is no significant change in the nature and economic circumstances of the investment. Hence, there is no justification for remeasurement of the existing ownership interest at the time of the increase. Rather the investor applies an accumulated cost approach equivalent to Approach 2 discussed in section 6.2.3.A above; i.e., the purchase price paid for the additional interest is added to the existing carrying amount of the associate or joint venture and the existing interest in the associate or joint venture is not remeasured. IAS 28.32 (see section 5 above) establishes the requirement that the cost of an investment in an associate or joint venture is allocated between the share of the fair value of net identifiable assets and the goodwill. This requirement is not limited to the initial application of equity accounting but applies to each acquisition of an investment. However, this requirement does not result in any revaluation of the existing share of net assets in a step increase transaction without a change in status. Investments in associates and joint ventures Rather, the existing ownership interests are accounted for under IAS 28.10 and IAS 28.32, whereby the carrying value is adjusted only for the investor’s share of the associate or joint venture’s profits or losses and other recognised equity transactions. Although, Illustration 6-4 below illustrates an increase in ownership of an associate that continues to be an associate, the accounting would be the same if the transaction had been an increase in ownership of a joint venture. Illustration 6-4: Accounting for an increase in the ownership of an associate Entity A obtains significant influence over Entity B by acquiring an investment of 25% at a cost of CU3,000. At the date of the acquisition, the fair value of the associate’s net identifiable assets is CU10,000, which equals their carrying amounts. Therefore, the difference between the amount paid of CU3,000 and A’s share of the fair value of B’s net assets of CU2,500 is attributable entirely to goodwill. The investment is accounted for under the equity method in the consolidated financial statements of A. Two years later, A acquires an additional investment of 20% in B at a cost of CU4,000, increasing its total investment in B to 45%. The investment is still an associate and accounted for using the equity method of accounting. For the purposes of the example, directly attributable costs have been ignored and it is assumed that no profit or loss arose during the period since the acquisition of the first 25%. Therefore, the carrying amount of the investment immediately prior to the additional investment is CU3,000. However, an asset held by the associate has increased in value by CU5,000 so that the fair value of the associate’s net identifiable assets is now CU15,000. To summarise, amounts are as follows: CU Fair value of net identifiable assets of Entity B at acquisition 10,000 Increase in fair value 5,000 Fair value of net identifiable assets of Entity B two years later 15,000 Because of the additional investment, the equity-accounted amount for the associate increases by CU4,000. The notional goodwill applicable to the second tranche of the acquisition is CU1,000 [CU4,000 - (20% × CU15,000)]. The impact of the additional investment on A’s equity-accounted amount for B is summarised as follows: % held Carrying amount Share of net identifiable assets Goodwill included in investment CU CU CU Existing investment 3,000 2,500 Additional investment 4,000 3,000 1,000 Total investment 7,000 5,500 1,500 The accounting for an increase in an associate or a joint venture that becomes a subsidiary is discussed in # Chapter 10 at section 9. 6.2.5 Existing associate that becomes a joint venture, or vice versa In the situations discussed in section 6.2.4 above, the acquisition of the additional interests did not result in a change in status of the investee, i.e., the associate remained an associate or the joint venture remained a joint venture. However, an associate could become a joint venture, either by the acquisition of an additional interest, or through a contractual agreement that gives the investor joint control. Alternatively, a contractual agreement could end, or part of an interest could be disposed of and a joint venture could become an associate. In all these situations, IAS 28 requires that the entity continues to apply the equity method and does not remeasure the retained interest. [IAS 28.24]. Therefore, the accounting described in Illustration 6-4 above would apply. Investments in associates and joint ventures 6.2.6 Common control transactions involving sales of associates IFRS 3 and IAS 28 are not clear about how to apply the equity method of accounting when an investor acquires an investment in an associate from an entity that is under common control. Therefore, there is an interpretation of whether, and how, to apply IFRS 3 principles to investments in associates. How we see it We believe there are two possible approaches when an investor acquires an investment in an associate from an entity that is under common control: acquisition accounting and pooling of interests. An entity accounts for such transactions using a consistent accounting policy. These approaches are discussed further in Chapter 11 at section 5. 6.3 Share of the investee 6.3.1 Accounting for potential voting rights In applying the equity method and determining the share of the investee’s earnings or losses to be recognised, the proportionate share of the associate or joint venture to be accounted for is generally based on the investor’s ownership interest in the ordinary shares. This is also generally the case when the investor holds potential voting rights, or other derivatives containing potential voting rights, in addition to the single investment in the ordinary shares. This is because an entity’s interest in an associate or a joint venture is determined solely based on existing ownership interests and does not reflect the possible exercise or conversion of potential voting rights and other derivative instruments. [IAS 28.12]. However, as an exception to this, IAS 28 recognises that: [IAS 28.13] The standard does not provide an example of such circumstances, but the assessment of whether a potential voting right or other derivative instrument gives the holder present access to returns associated with the ownership interest in the shares is discussed in Chapter 8 at sections 6.1, 6.2.2 and 6.3 in the context of options over NCI. Similar considerations apply to the assessment of whether a potential voting right or other derivative instrument gives the holder present access to returns associated with the ownership interest in the shares under IAS 28. IFRS 9 does not apply to interests in associates and joint ventures that are accounted for using the equity method. When instruments containing potential voting rights in substance currently give access to the returns associated with an ownership interest in an associate or a joint venture, the instruments are not subject to IFRS 9. In all other cases, instruments containing potential voting rights in an associate or a joint venture are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9. [IAS 28.14]. Once the potential voting rights are exercised and the share in the investee increases, the fair value of such instruments at the exercise date is part of the cost to be recognised in accounting for the step increase (see section 6.2.3 above). 6.3.2 Cumulative preference shares held by parties other than the investor If an associate or joint venture has outstanding cumulative preference shares that are held by parties other than the investor and that are classified as equity, the investor computes its share of profits or losses after adjusting for the dividends on such shares, whether or not the dividends have been declared. [IAS 28.37]. Although Illustration 6-5 below illustrates cumulative preference shares issued by an associate, the accounting would be the same if the shares were issued by a joint venture. Extract from IAS 28 In some circumstances, an entity has, in substance, an existing ownership as a result of a transaction that currently gives it access to the returns associated with an ownership interest. In such circumstances, the proportion allocated to the entity is determined by taking into account the eventual exercise of those potential voting rights and other derivative instruments that currently give the entity access to the returns. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 6-5: Cumulative preference shares issued by an associate An entity holds an investment of 30% in the ordinary shares of an associate that has net assets of CU200,000 and net profit for the year of CU24,500. The associate has issued 5,000 cumulative preference shares with a nominal value of CU10 which entitle its holders to a 9% cumulative preference dividend. The cumulative preference shares are classified by the associate as equity in accordance with the requirements of IAS 32 Financial Instruments: Presentation. The associate has not declared dividends on the cumulative preference shares in the past two years. The investor calculates its share of the associate’s net assets and net profit as follows: CU Net assets 200,000 9% Cumulative preference shares (50,000) Undeclared dividend on cumulative preference shares 2 years × 9% × CU50,000 = (9,000) Net assets value attributable to ordinary shareholders 141,000 Investor’s 30% share of the net assets 42,300 Net profit for the year 24,500 Share of profit of holders of cumulative preference shares 9% of CU50,000 = (4,500) Net profit attributable to ordinary shareholders 20,000 Investor’s 30% share of the net profit 6,000 If the investor also owned all the cumulative preference shares, then it would assess whether those preference shares are accounted for under IFRS 9 or IAS 28 (see section 6.3.1 above). Assuming that the preference shares are accounted for under IAS 28, the investor’s share in the net assets of the associate would be CU42,300 + CU50,000 + CU9,000 = CU101,300. Its share in the net profit would be **CU6,000 + CU4,500 = CU10,500.** 6.3.3 Several classes of equity and substantive profit sharing arrangements When an associate or joint venture has a complicated equity structure with several classes of equity shares that have varying entitlements to net profits, equity or liquidation preferences, or substantive profit sharing arrangements, the investor needs to assess carefully the rights attaching to each class of equity and the arrangements in determining the appropriate amount to recognise for its share of the investee’s profit or losses. 6.3.4 Where the reporting entity is a group (indirect interests) A group’s share in an associate or joint venture is the aggregate of the holdings in that associate or joint venture by the parent and its subsidiaries. The holdings of the group’s other associates or joint ventures are ignored for this purpose. [IAS 28.27]. Illustration 6-6 below illustrates the group’s share in an associate when investments are also held by other entities in the group. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 6-6: Share in an associate or a joint venture when the reporting entity is a group Parent A holds a 100% investment in Subsidiary B, which in turn holds a 25% investment in Associate Z. In addition, A also holds a 30% investment in Associate C and a 50% investment in Joint Venture D, each of which holds a 10% investment in Z. In its consolidated financial statements, A accounts for a 25% investment in Z under the equity method because: • The investments in Z held by C and D are not considered • A consolidates the assets of B, which include a 25% investment in Z A’s indirect interests in Z held via its interests in C and D are accounted for via its accounting for C and D. 6.3.5 Where the investee is a group: non-controlling interests in an associate’s or joint venture’s consolidated financial statements When an associate or joint venture itself has subsidiaries, the profits or losses, OCI and net assets considered in applying the equity method are those recognised in the associate’s or joint venture’s consolidated financial statements, but after any adjustments necessary to give effect to uniform accounting policies (see section 8.2 below). [IAS 28.27]. An associate or joint venture might not own all the shares in some of its subsidiaries, in which case its consolidated financial statements will include non-controlling interests (NCI). Under IFRS 10, any NCI are presented in the consolidated statement of financial position within equity, separately from the equity of the owners of the parent. Profit or loss and each component of OCI are attributed to the owners of the parent and to the NCI. [IFRS 10.22, IFRS 10.B94]. The profit or loss and OCI reported in the associate’s or joint venture’s consolidated financial statements will include 100% of the amounts relating to the subsidiaries, but the overall profit or loss and total comprehensive income will be split between the amounts attributable to the owners of the parent (i.e., the associate or joint venture) and those attributable to the NCI. The net assets in the consolidated statement of financial position will also include 100% of the amounts relating to the subsidiaries, with any NCI in the net assets presented in the consolidated statement of financial position within equity, separately from the equity of the owners of the parent. IAS 28 does not explicitly say whether the investor bases the accounting for its share of the associate’s or joint venture’s profits, OCI and net assets under the equity method on the amounts before or after any NCI in the associate’s or joint venture’s consolidated accounts. However, the implementation guidance to IAS 1 indicates that the amounts disclosed for ‘share of profits of associates’ and ‘share of other comprehensive income of associates’ represent the amounts “attributable to owners of the associates, i.e., it is after tax and non-controlling interests in the associates”.12 How we see it Therefore, in our view, when an investee does not wholly own its subsidiaries, since the investor’s interest in the associate or joint venture is as an owner of the parent, its share of its associate’s or joint venture’s profits is based on the amounts that are reported as attributable to the owners of the parent (controlling interest) in the associate’s or joint venture’s consolidated financial statements, i.e., after any amounts attributable to the NCI. IAS 1 IG6 XYZ Group - Statement of profit or loss and other comprehensive income for the year ended 31 December 20X7 (illustrating the presentation of profit and loss and other comprehensive income in one statement and the classification of expenses within profit by function). Investments in associates and joint ventures Transactions between the reporting entity and its associates or joint ventures 7.1 Elimination of ‘upstream’ and ‘downstream’ transactions IAS 28 requires certain eliminations of transactions between an investor and its associate. Extract from IAS 28 Gains and losses resulting from ‘upstream’ and ‘downstream’ transactions between an entity (including its consolidated subsidiaries) and its associate or joint venture are recognised in the entity’s financial statements only to the extent of unrelated investors’ interests in the associate or joint venture. ‘Upstream’ transactions are, for example, sales of assets from an associate or a joint venture to the investor. ‘Downstream’ transactions are, for example, sales or contributions of assets from the investor to its associate or its joint venture. The investor’s share in the associate’s or joint venture’s gains or losses resulting from these transactions is eliminated. **[IAS 28.28].** How we see it We believe that an appropriate approach for presenting these entries is as follows: • In the statement of profit or loss and other comprehensive income, the adjustment is taken against either the investor’s profit or the share of the associate’s or joint venture’s profit, whichever is the entity that recorded the profit on the transaction. • In the statement of financial position, the adjustment is made against the asset which was the subject of the transaction if it is held by the investor, or against the carrying amount of the associate or joint venture if the asset is held by the associate or joint venture. However, certain of these presentations have drawbacks, discussed in more detail below. The selected accounting policy must be consistently applied and disclosed in the financial statements. This approach is consistent with the approach required by IAS 28 in dealing with the elimination of unrealised gains and losses arising on contributions of non-monetary assets to an associate or joint venture in exchange for an equity interest in the associate or joint venture (see section 7.5 below). Illustration 7-1 and Illustration 7-2 below show our suggested approach to this requirement of IAS 28. Although these examples illustrate transactions between the reporting entity and an associate, the accounting would be the same if the transactions occurred between the reporting entity and a joint venture. Illustration 7-1: Elimination of profit on sale by investor to associate (‘downstream transaction’) One month before its reporting date, Entity H sells inventory costing CU750,000 to Associate A for CU1m, all of which remains in A’s inventory at the reporting date. H owns 40% of A. H recorded revenue of CU1m and cost of sales of CU750,000, a profit of CU250,000. However since, at the reporting date, the inventory is still held by A, only 60% of this transaction is regarded by IAS 28 as having taken place (in effect with the other shareholders of A). This is reflected by the elimination accounting entry: Dr Revenue **CU400,000** Cr Cost of sales **CU300,000** Cr Investment in A **CU100,000** This entry effectively defers recognition of 40% of the profit on sale (CU250,000 × 40%) and offsets the deferred profit against the carrying amount of H’s investment in A. After the reporting date, when the inventory is sold to a third party for CU1.2m by A, this deferred profit can be released by H, reflected by the following equity accounting entry: Dr Investment in A **CU100,000** Dr Cost of sales **CU300,000** Cr Revenue **CU400,000** Investments in associates and joint ventures An alternative approach would be to eliminate the profit on 40% of the sale against the cost of sales. Before the reporting date, this is reflected by the equity accounting entry: Dr Cost of sales **CU100,000** Cr Investment in A **CU100,000** After the reporting date, when the inventory is sold to a third party for CU1.2m by A, this deferred profit can be released by H, reflected by the following equity accounting entry: Dr Investment in A **CU100,000** Cr Cost of sales **CU100,000** An argument in favour of the alternative approach is that the revenue figures are not adjusted because the sales to associates or joint ventures are disclosed as related party transactions. However, this approach causes volatility in H’s reported gross margin, as revenue and the related net margin are not recognised in the same accounting period. Illustration 7-2: Elimination of profit on sale by associate to reporting entity (‘upstream transaction’) This is the mirror image of the transaction in Illustration 7-1 above. Before H’s reporting date, A sells inventory costing CU750,000 to H for CU1m. A recognised a profit of CU250,000 (CU1m - CU750,000), 40% of which (CU100,000) is regarded under IAS 28 as unrealised by H, and is therefore deferred and offset against inventory: Dr Equity method earnings in A **CU100,000** Cr Inventory **CU100,000** In the following period, when the inventory is sold to a third party for CU1.2m by H, the entry is reversed: Dr Inventory **CU100,000** Cr Equity method earnings in A **CU100,000** Under this approach, at the reporting date, the investment in A will have increased by CU100,000 more than the share of profit of associates as reported in group profit or loss (and in the following period by CU100,000 less). This is because the adjustment to the statement of financial position at the reporting date is to inventory rather than the carrying value of the investment in A, reflecting the fact that A has, indeed, made a profit. It might therefore be necessary to disclose the reasons for this difference in the notes to the financial statements. A transaction between an investor and its associate or joint venture could indicate a reduction in the net realisable value or an impairment loss of the asset that is the subject of the transaction. IAS 28 requires that when this is the case in a downstream transaction, those losses are recognised in full by the investor. Similarly, when an upstream transaction provides evidence of a reduction in the net realisable value of the assets to be purchased or of an impairment loss of those assets, the investor recognises its share of those losses. [IAS 28.29]. The effect of these requirements is shown in Illustration 7-3 and Illustration 7-4 below. Although these examples illustrate transactions between the reporting entity and a joint venture, the accounting would be the same if the transactions occurred between the reporting entity and an associate. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 7-3: Sale of asset from venturer to joint venture at a loss Two entities, A and B, establish Joint Venture C in which A and B each hold 50%. A and B each contribute CU5m in cash to C in exchange for equity shares. C then uses CU8m of its CU10m cash to acquire a property from A recorded in the financial statements of A at CU10m. CU8m is agreed to be the fair value of the property. The required accounting entry by A is as follows: CU m CU m Dr Cash(1) Dr Investment in C(2) Dr Loss on sale(3) Cr Property(4) (1) CU8m received from C less CU5m contributed to C. (2) Represented by 50% of C’s cash CU2m (CU10m from A and B minus CU8m to A), plus 50% of CU8m (carrying value of the property in books of C). (3) Loss on sale of property CU2m (CU8m received from C less CU10m carrying value = CU2m) recognised in full because the transaction indicated an impairment of the property. In effect, it is the result that would have been obtained if A had recognised an impairment charge immediately prior to the sale and then recognised no gain or loss on the sale. (4) Derecognition of A’s original property. Illustration 7-4: Sale of asset from joint venture to venturer at a loss On 1 January 20X2, two entities A and B establish Joint Venture C in which A and B each hold 50%. A and B each contribute CU5m in cash to C in exchange for equity shares. C then uses CU8m of its CU10m cash to acquire a property from an independent third-party D. Property prices fall during 20X2 and on 31 December 20X2, the property is sold to A for CU7m settled in cash, which is agreed to be its fair value. The required accounting entry by A for both transactions combined is as follows: CU m CU m Dr Property (1) 7.0 Dr Investment in C(2) 4.5 Dr Share of loss of C(3) 0.5 Cr Cash (4) 12.0 (1) CU7m paid to C. (2) Represented by 50% of C’s cash CU9m (CU10m from A and B minus CU8m to D plus CU7m received from A). (3) Loss in C’s books is CU1m (CU8m cost of property less CU7m proceeds of sale). A recognises its 50% share because the transaction indicates an impairment of the asset, which is recognised by C as an impairment charge immediately prior to the sale. (4) CU5m cash contributed to C plus CU7m consideration for property. 7.1.1 Elimination of ‘downstream’ unrealised profits in excess of the investment Occasionally an investor’s share of the unrealised profit on the sale of an asset to an associate or a joint venture exceeds the carrying value of the investment held. In that case, to what extent is any profit in excess of the carrying value of the investment eliminated? IAS 28 is unclear about the elimination of ‘downstream’ unrealised gains in excess of the investment. Consequently, the IFRS IC received a request asking for clarification of the accounting treatment when the amount of gains to eliminate in a ‘downstream’ transaction in accordance with IAS 28.28 exceeds the amount of the entity’s interest in the joint venture. The request specifically asked whether: • The gain from the transaction is eliminated only to the extent that it does not exceed the carrying amount of the entity’s interest in the joint venture Or • The remaining gain in excess of the carrying amount of the entity’s interest in the joint venture is also eliminated and if so, what it would be eliminated against Investments in associates and joint ventures The IFRS IC determined that the entity eliminates the gain from a ‘downstream’ transaction to the extent of the related investor’s interest in the joint venture, even if the gain to be eliminated exceeds the carrying amount of the entity’s interest in the joint venture, as required by IAS 28.28. Any eliminated gain that is in excess of the carrying amount of the entity’s interest in the joint venture is recognised as deferred income.13 In July 2013, the IASB tentatively agreed with the views of the IFRS IC and directed the staff to draft amendments to IAS 28.14 However, in June 2015, the IASB tentatively decided to defer further work on this topic to the equity method project. This is discussed further in section 13 below. How we see it We believe that, until the IASB issues an amendment to IAS 28, the investor can either recognise the excess, referred to above, as ‘deferred income’ or restrict the elimination to the amount required to reduce the investment to zero. The treatment chosen is based on the investor’s accounting policy choice for dealing with other situations when IAS 28 is unclear, reflecting whether the investor considers the equity method of accounting to be primarily a method of consolidation (which would be consistent with recognition of the excess as deferred income) or a method of valuing an investment (which would be consistent with restricting the elimination to the amount that reduces the investment to zero). The investor applies a consistent policy to such situations. 7.1.1.A Method of consolidation approach This approach gives precedence to the requirements in IAS 28.26, which is also consistent with the general requirement to apply IFRS 10 consolidation elimination principles. [IAS 28.26]. Although IAS 28.38 requires an investor to discontinue application of the equity method when an investor’s share of losses equals or exceeds its interest in the associate (see section 8.3 below), [IAS 28.38], the elimination does not represent a real ‘loss’ to the investor but is simply the non-recognition of a gain because of normal consolidation principles. Therefore, IAS 28.38 is subordinate to the requirement to eliminate unrealised profits. 7.1.1.B Method of valuing investment approach This approach views the requirements of IAS 28.38 as taking precedence over the requirements of IAS 28.28 to eliminate unrealised profits from a transaction between the investor and the associate. The elimination of the full amount of the share of unrealised profit effectively results in the recognition of a ‘loss’ to the investor. Furthermore, by deferring the ‘loss’, the investor is effectively recognising a negative investment balance, which is not permitted or required under IAS 28 when the investor does not have any further legal or constructive obligations in relation to the asset or the associate. 7.1.1.C Examples Although the following Illustrations show transactions between the reporting entity and an associate, the accounting would be the same if the transactions occurred between the reporting entity and a joint venture. IFRIC Update, May 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. *Reference: IASB Update, July 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 7-5: Elimination of downstream unrealised profits in excess of the investment An investor has a 40% investment in an associate, which it carries in its statement of financial position at CU800,000. The investor sells a property to the associate in exchange for cash, which results in a profit of CU3m. After the sale, 40% of that profit (i.e., CU1.2m) is unrealised from the investor’s perspective. It is assumed that the requirements for determining when a performance obligation is satisfied in IFRS 15 Revenue from Contracts with Customers have been met. Great care needs to be taken when the investor has any continuing involvement with the transferred asset - for example, guaranteeing limited recourse debt funding used to acquire the asset - to ensure that the requirements in IFRS 15 have been satisfied for revenue to be recognised. The two approaches for determining to what extent a profit in excess of the carrying value of the investment are eliminated are as follows: Method of consolidation approach - excess of the unrealised profit over the carrying value of the investment recognised as ‘deferred income’ The investor eliminates the investor’s total share of the unrealised profit against the carrying amount of the investment in the associate until reaching zero, recognising the excess as a ‘deferred income’ or similar balance, as follows: Dr Profit on sale of property(1) **CU1,200,000** Cr Investment in associate **CU800,000** Cr ‘Deferred income’ **CU400,000** (1) This adjustment could be presented in the income statement as an adjustment to revenue and cost of sales or to cost of sales only as discussed at Illustration 7-1 above. This leaves a net profit of CU1.8m recognised in the consolidated financial statements. The investor recognises deferred income as the asset or the investment in the associate is realised (e.g., upon disposal of the investor’s investment in the associate, or upon the disposal or depreciation of the asset by the associate). Method of valuing investment approach - restricts the elimination to the amount required to reduce the investment to zero If the investor does not have any further legal or constructive obligations in relation to the asset or the associate, no liability exists, and no further profit is deferred. The investor eliminates the unrealised profit to the extent that it reduces the carrying value of the investment to zero, as follows: Dr Profit on sale of property(1) **CU800,000** Cr Investment in associate **CU800,000** (1) This adjustment could be presented in the statement of profit or loss and other comprehensive income as an adjustment to revenue and cost of sales or to cost of sales only as discussed at Illustration 7-1 above. This leaves a net profit of CU2,200,000 recognised in the consolidated financial statements. The investor does not recognise further profits in the associate until they exceed the unrecognised unrealised profits of **CU400,000.** 7.1.2 Transactions between associates and/or joint ventures Transactions could take place between associates and/or joint ventures held by the same investor. Although IAS 28.28 refers only to upstream and downstream transactions between an investor and its associate or its joint venture, we consider these to be examples of typical transactions to be eliminated as a result of the requirements of IAS 28.26 which says that “Many of the procedures that are appropriate for the application of the equity method are similar to the consolidation procedures described in IFRS 10”. They are not the only transactions requiring elimination as a result of applying the principle in IAS 28.26. Therefore, applying the principles in IAS 28.28 and IFRS 10.B86, the unrealised profit in the investor’s financial statements arising from any transaction between the associates (and/or joint ventures) is eliminated to the extent of the related investor’s interests in the associates (and/or joint ventures) as appropriate. How we see it When transactions take place between associates and/or joint ventures, which are accounted for under the equity method, we believe the investor applies the requirements of IAS 28 and IFRS 10 by analogy and eliminate its share of any unrealised profits or losses. In practice, however, it might be difficult to determine whether such transactions have taken place. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 7-6: Elimination of profits and losses resulting from transactions between associates and/or joint ventures Entity H has a 25% interest in Associate A and a 30% interest in Joint Venture B. During the reporting period, A sold inventory costing CU1m to B for CU1.2m. All of the inventory remains on B’s statement of financial position at the end of the reporting period. H eliminates CU15,000 (i.e., 30% × 25% × CU200,000) as its share of the profit that is unrealised. 7.2 Reciprocal interests Reciprocal interests (or ‘cross-holdings’) arise when an associate or joint venture itself holds an investment in the reporting entity. Although the discussion below refers to a reciprocal interest between the reporting entity and an associate, the discussion below would apply equally to a joint venture. 7.2.1 Reciprocal interests in reporting entity accounted for under the equity method by the associate When the associate’s investment in the reporting entity is such that the associate in turn has significant influence over the reporting entity and accounts for that investment under the equity method, a literal interpretation of IAS 28.27 is that an investor records its share of an associate’s profits and net assets, including the associate’s equity accounted profits and net assets of its investment in the investor. The reciprocal interests can therefore give rise to a measure of double counting of profits and net assets between the investor and its associate. The elimination of reciprocal interests was discussed by the IFRS IC in August 2002. The IFRS IC agreed not to require publication of an Interpretation on this issue but did state that “like the consolidation procedures applied when a subsidiary is consolidated, the equity method requires reciprocal interests to be eliminated.”15 How we see it Therefore, we believe that the requirement in IFRS 10.B86 to eliminate intragroup balances, transactions, income and expenses is applied by analogy when reciprocal interests exist. Neither IFRS 10 nor IAS 28 explains how an entity eliminates the double counting that arises from reciprocal holdings. We believe that a direct holding only (or net approach) is applicable, whereby the profit of the investor is calculated by adding its direct investment in the associate to its trading profits, as shown in Illustration 7-7 below. This method takes up the investor’s share of the associate’s profits excluding the equity income arising on the reciprocal shareholdings and eliminates only the effects of an entity’s indirect investment in its own shares. The investor also eliminates the reciprocal interest in calculating earnings per share (EPS), consistent with the treatment of treasury shares. The associate is not part of the group and therefore, the shares held in the investor are not ‘treasury shares’ as defined. However, applying IAS 28.26, the reciprocal shareholdings are eliminated as they would be if a subsidiary held shares in its parent, under IFRS 10.B86(c). IFRIC Update, August 2002, p.3, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Investments in associates and joint ventures Illustration 7-7: Elimination of equity-accounted reciprocal interests Entity A has a 40% equity interest in Entity B and conversely, B has a 30% interest in A. The structure of the reciprocal holdings is shown in the diagram below: Entity A Share in equity of B 40% Shares in A held by ‘outside’ shareholders 70% Trading profit of A (before share in profit of B) **CU60,000** Net assets of A (before share in net assets of B) **CU600,000** Number of shares in issue 100,000 Entity B Share in equity of A 30% Shares in B held by ‘outside’ shareholders 60% Trading profit of B (before share in profit of A) **CU110,000** Net assets of B (before share in net assets of A) **CU1,100,000** Number of shares in issue 40,000 Income The profit for the period is calculated by adding the direct interest in the associate’s profit: • Profit of A: Trading profit of A + 40% × trading profit of B = CU60,000 + 40% × CU110,000 = CU104,000 • Profit of B: Trading profit of B + 30% × trading profit of A = CU110,000 + 30% × CU60,000 = CU128,000 Statement of financial position A similar approach can be applied to calculate the net assets of A and B: • Net assets of A including share in B without eliminations: CU600,000 + 40% × CU1.1m = CU1.04m • Net assets of B including share in A without eliminations: CU1.1m + 30% × CU600,000 = CU1.28m EPS It is necessary to adjust the number of shares to eliminate the reciprocal holdings: For A it can be argued that it indirectly owns 40% of B’s 30% interest, i.e., A indirectly owns 12% (= 40% × 30%) of its own shares. Those shares are ignored for the purposes of the EPS calculation. • Number of A shares after elimination of ‘treasury shares’: 100,000 × (100% - 12%) = 88,000 shares Similarly, B indirectly owns 30% of A’s 40% interest, i.e., B indirectly owns 12% (= 30% × 40%) of its own shares. • Number of B shares after elimination of ‘treasury shares’: 40,000 × (100% - 12%) = 35,200 shares Investments in associates and joint ventures The EPS for the shareholders of A and B is calculated as follows: • EPS A: **CU104,000 ÷ 88,000 = CU1.18** • EPS B: **CU128,000 ÷ 35,200 = CU3.64** The EPS is equivalent to the hypothetical dividend per share in the case of full distribution of all profits. Conclusion The financial statements reflect both the interests of the ‘outside’ shareholders and the interests that B shareholders have in A. It is worthwhile noting that the combined underlying trading profit of A and B is only CU170,000 (i.e., CU60,000 + CU110,000), whereas their combined reported profit is CU232,000 (i.e., CU104,000 + CU128,000). Similarly, the combined underlying net assets of A and B are only CU1,700,000, whereas the combined reported net assets are CU2,320,000. 7.2.2 Reciprocal interests in reporting entity not accounted for under the equity method by the associate In some situations, the associate’s investment in the reporting entity is such that the associate does not have significant influence over the reporting entity and accounts for that investment under IFRS 9, either as at fair value through OCI or at fair value through profit or loss. Although the associate is not applying the equity method, the reciprocal interest can still result in double counting of profits and net assets between the investor and its associate when the investor accounts for its share of the profits and net assets of the associate. Again, IAS 28.26 states that many of the procedures appropriate for the application of the equity method are similar to the consolidation procedures described in IFRS 10. How we see it In our view, the requirement in IFRS 10.B86 to eliminate intragroup balances, transactions, income and expenses is applied by analogy to reciprocal interests in the reporting entity accounted for under IFRS 9. Accordingly, in its equity accounting, the investor eliminates income from the associate’s investment in the investor. This elimination includes dividends and changes in fair value recognised either in profit or loss or OCI. The investor also eliminates the reciprocal interest in calculating EPS. Illustration 7-8: Elimination of reciprocal interests not accounted for under the equity method Investor A has a 20% interest in an Associate B. B has a 10% interest in A, which does not give rise to significant influence. Scenario 1 B recognises a profit of CU1,300 for the year, which includes a dividend of CU100 received from A and a gain of CU200 from measuring its investment in A at fair value through profit or loss. In this scenario, A’s equity method share of B’s profit is CU200, being 20% of B’s profit of CU1,000 after excluding income (dividend of CU100 plus fair value gain of CU200) on its investment in A. Scenario 2 B recognises a profit of CU1,100 for the year, which includes a dividend of CU100 received from A and recognises CU200 in OCI from measuring its investment in A as a financial asset at fair value through OCI. In this scenario, A’s equity method share of B’s profit is CU200, being 20% of B’s profit of CU1,000 after excluding income (dividend of CU100) on its investment in A. A’s share of B’s OCI also excludes the gain of CU200 recognised in OCI arising from its investment in A. In both scenarios, A also reduces its equity balance and its investment in B by its effective 2% interest in its own shares and uses the reduced number of shares in determining EPS. Investments in associates and joint ventures 7.3 Loans and borrowings between the reporting entity and its associates or joint ventures The requirement in IAS 28.28 to eliminate partially unrealised profits or losses on transactions with associates or joint ventures is expressed in terms of transactions involving the transfer of assets. The requirement for partial elimination of profits could be read to not apply to items such as interest paid on loans and borrowings between the reporting entity and its associates or joint ventures since such loans and borrowings do not involve the transfer of assets giving rise to gains or losses. Moreover, they are not normally regarded as part of the investor’s share of the net assets of the associate or joint venture, but as separate transactions (except in the case of loss-making associates or joint ventures, see section 8.3 below). How we see it We believe that loans and borrowings between the reporting entity and its associates or joint ventures generally are not eliminated in the reporting entity’s consolidated financial statements when the interest income and expense are recognised by both parties. However, if the associate or joint venture has capitalised the borrowing costs then the investor would need to eliminate a relevant share of its interest income and realise it as the associate depreciates the qualifying asset. The same principle applies to eliminate a share of capitalised management or advisory fees charged to an associate or joint venture. 7.4 Statement of cash flows Changes in cash and cash equivalents relating to associates or joint ventures accounted for under the equity method affect the entity’s statement of cash flows only to the extent of the cash flows between the group and the investee (for example, to dividends and advances). [IAS 7.37, IAS 7.38]. The same concept applies to associates or joint ventures carried at fair value (see section 3 above). In the consolidated statement of cash flows, no elimination adjustment is made in respect of the cash flows relating to transactions with associates or joint ventures. This contrasts with the requirement in any consolidated statement of cash flows to eliminate the cash flows between members of the group. 7.5 Contributions of non-monetary assets to an associate or a joint venture It is fairly common for an entity to create or change its interest in an associate or a joint venture by contributing some of the entity’s existing non-monetary assets to that associate or joint venture. This raises several questions as to how such transactions are accounted for, in particular whether they are accounted for at book value or fair value. IAS 28 requires the contribution of a non-monetary asset to an associate or a joint venture in exchange for an equity interest in the associate or joint venture to be accounted for in accordance with IAS 28.28, except when the contribution lacks commercial substance, as described in IAS 16 (see section 7.5.1 below and Chapter 19 at section 4.4). [IAS 28.30]. IAS 28.28 requires gains and losses resulting from transactions between an entity and its associate or joint venture to be recognised only to the extent of unrelated interests in the associate or joint venture. The investor’s share in the associate’s or joint venture’s gains or losses resulting from those transactions is eliminated (see section 7.1 above for a discussion of the requirements relating to these transactions). However, there is a conflict between the requirements of IAS 28 and the requirements in IFRS 10 relating to accounting for the loss of control of a subsidiary, when a subsidiary is contributed by the investor to an associate or joint venture and control of the subsidiary is consequently lost. This conflict is discussed below in section 7.5.3. If a contribution lacks commercial substance, the gain or loss is regarded as unrealised and is not recognised unless IAS 28.31 (see below) also applies. The unrealised gains and losses are eliminated against the investment accounted for using the equity method and are not presented as deferred gains or losses in the entity’s consolidated statement of financial position or in the entity’s statement of financial position in which investments are accounted for using the equity method. [IAS 28.30]. When ‘unrealised’ losses are eliminated in this way, the effect will be to apply what is sometimes referred to as ‘asset swap’ accounting. In other words, the carrying value of the investment in the associate or joint venture will be the same as the carrying value of the non-monetary assets transferred in exchange for it, subject to any necessary provision for impairment uncovered by the transaction. Investments in associates and joint ventures If, in addition to receiving an equity interest in an associate or a joint venture, an entity receives monetary or non-monetary assets, the entity recognises in full, in profit or loss, the portion of the gain or loss on the non-monetary contribution relating to the monetary or non-monetary assets received. [IAS 28.31]. If the terms of a transaction provide evidence that the entity’s interest in the associate, or the asset that was contributed, might be impaired, a charge is recognised, as discussed in section 7.1 above and in section 7.5.4 below. 7.5.1 ‘Commercial substance’ IAS 28 requires that a transaction is not treated as realised when it lacks commercial substance as described in IAS 16. [IAS 28.30]. Extract from IAS 16 An entity determines whether an exchange transaction has commercial substance by considering the extent to which its future cash flows are expected to change as a result of the transaction. An exchange transaction has commercial substance if: (a) the configuration (risk, timing and amount) of the cash flows of the asset received differs from the configuration of the cash flows of the asset transferred; or (b) the entity-specific value of the portion of the entity’s operations affected by the transaction changes as a result of the exchange; and (c) the difference in (a) or (b) is significant relative to the fair value of the assets exchanged. For the purpose of determining whether an exchange transaction has commercial substance, the entity-specific value of the portion of the entity’s operations affected by the transaction shall reflect post-tax cash flows. The result of these analyses may be clear without an entity having to perform detailed calculations. **[IAS 16.25].** The Board included the ‘commercial substance’ test in IAS 16 in order to “help to give users … assurance that the substance of a transaction in which the acquired asset is measured at fair value (and often, consequentially, a gain on the disposal of the transferred asset is recognised in income) is the same as its legal form.” [IAS 16.25, IAS 16.BC21]. The first step when assessing whether a transaction has commercial substance is to determine the cash flows both of the asset given up and of the asset acquired (the latter being the interest in the associate or joint venture). This determination could be sufficient by itself to satisfy (a) above, as it might be obvious that there are significant differences in the configuration of the cash flows. The type of income might have changed. For example, if the entity contributed a non-monetary asset such as a property or intangible asset to the associate or joint venture, the reporting entity might now be receiving a rental or royalty stream from the associate or joint venture, whereas previously the asset contributed to the cash flows of the cash-generating unit of which it was a part. However, determining the cash flows might not result in a clear-cut conclusion, in which case the entity- specific value will have to be calculated. This is not the same as a value in use calculation under IAS 36 Impairment of Assets, in that the entity can use a discount rate based on its own assessment of the risks specific to the operations, not those that reflect current market assessments, [IAS 16.BC22], and post-tax cash flows. [IAS 16.25]. The calculation might not be highly sensitive to the discount rate as the same rate is used to calculate the entity-specific value of both the asset surrendered and the entity’s interest in the associate or joint venture. However, if the entity considers that a high discount rate is appropriate, this will have an impact on whether or not the difference is significant relative to the fair value of the assets exchanged. The transaction will have commercial substance if the entity-specific value of both the asset surrendered and the entity’s interest in the associate or joint venture are not only different to one another but also significant compared to the fair values of the assets exchanged. [IAS 16.25]. How we see it We believe that transactions entered into with genuine commercial purposes in mind are likely to pass the ‘commercial substance’ tests outlined in IAS 16.25 above. Testing transactions for their commercial substance will require entities to focus on the fair value of the transaction as a whole and not to follow the strict legal form. Investments in associates and joint ventures 7.5.2 Contributions of non-monetary assets - IAS 28 approach IAS 28 does not give an example of the accounting treatment that it envisages when a gain is treated as ‘realised’. We believe that the intended approach is that set out in Illustration 7-9 below. In essence, this approach reflects the fact that the reporting entity: (a) Has acquired an interest in an associate or joint venture and therefore the entity’s share of the fair value of the net identifiable assets must be accounted for at fair value (see section 5 above) And (b) Is required by IAS 28 to restrict any gain arising from transactions between the reporting entity and its associate or joint venture relating to assets that remain on the books of the associate. Such gains are recognised only to the extent that the gain is attributable to the other investor in the associate or joint venture. This leads to an adjustment of the carrying amount of the investment in the associate or joint venture In Illustration 7-9 below, we show the accounting by Entity A when the non-monetary assets it has contributed are intangible assets that are not in a subsidiary. See section 7.5.3 below for guidance when the assets contributed are in a subsidiary. Although Illustration 7-9 below is based on a transaction resulting in the formation of a joint venture, the accounting treatment by Entity A would be the same if it had obtained an interest in an associate. Illustration 7-9: Contribution of non-monetary assets to form a joint venture A and B are two major pharmaceutical companies, which agree to form a joint venture (JV Co) as defined in IAS 28. A will own 40% of the joint venture, and B, 60%. The total fair value of the new business of JV Co is CU250m. A’s contribution to the venture is several intangible assets that are not in a subsidiary, with a carrying amount of CU60m. The fair value of the intangible assets contributed by A is CU100m, i.e., equivalent to 40% of the total fair value of JV Co of CU250m. B contributes a business in a subsidiary, with separable net identifiable assets of CU85m and goodwill of CU15m. The fair value of the separable net identifiable assets is CU120m. The fair value of the business contributed is CU150m (60% of total fair value of JV Co of CU250m). See section 7.5.3 below for guidance on B’s accounting. The book and fair values of the assets/businesses contributed by A and B can therefore be summarised as follows: A B (CU million) Book value Fair value Book value Fair value Intangible assets Separable net identifiable assets Goodwill Total IAS 28.32 requires A to recognise the identifiable assets at fair value upon the acquisition of its 40% interest in the new venture. However, as noted above, any gain or loss recognised by A must reflect only the disposal of the assets to the other partners in the venture (i.e., in this case, 60% - the extent to which A’s intangible assets are effectively transferred to B through B’s 60% interest in the new venture). [IAS 28.30, IAS 28.32]. This gives rise to the following accounting entry. CU m CU m Dr Investment in JV Co: • Share of net identifiable assets of JV Co (1) • Goodwill (2) Cr Intangible assets contributed to JV Co (3) Cr Gain on disposal (4) Investments in associates and joint ventures (1) 40% of fair value of separable net identifiable assets (including A’s intangible assets) of new entity CU88m (40% of [CU100m + CU120m] as in table above) less elimination of 40% of gain on disposal CU16m (40% of CU40m, being the difference between the book value [CU60m] and fair value [CU100m] of A’s intangible assets, as in table above, contributed to JV Co) = CU72m. This is equivalent to, and perhaps more easily calculated as, 40% of [book value of A’s intangible assets + fair value of B’s separable net identifiable assets], i.e., 40% × [CU60m + CU120m] = CU72m. Under the equity method, this CU72m together with the CU12m of goodwill (see (2) below) would be included as the equity accounted amount of JV Co. (2) Fair value of consideration given CU100m (as in table above) less fair value of 40% share of separable net identifiable assets of JV Co acquired CU88m (see (1) above) = CU12m. This is equivalent to, and perhaps more easily calculated as, 40% of the fair value of B’s goodwill, i.e., 40% × CU30m = CU12m. Under the equity method, as noted at (1) above, this CU12m together with the CU72m relating to the separable net identifiable assets would be included as the equity accounted amount of JV Co. (3) Previous carrying amount of intangible assets contributed by A, now deconsolidated. (4) Fair value of business acquired CU100m (40% of CU250m) less book value of intangible assets disposed of CU60m (as in table above) = CU40m, less 40% of gain eliminated (CU16m) = CU24m. The CU16m eliminated reduces A’s share of JV Co’s separable net identifiable assets by CU16m (see (1) above). It is common when joint ventures are set up in this way for the fair value of the assets contributed not to be exactly in proportion to the fair values of the venturers’ agreed relative shares. Cash ‘equalisation’ payments are then made between the venturers so that the overall financial position of the venturer does correspond to the agreed relative shares in the venture. How we see it We believe that an appropriate treatment of cash equalisation payments, in which a proportion of the gain on disposal is eliminated equal to the investor’s ownership interest, in the context of a transaction within the scope of IAS 28, is shown in Illustration 7-10 below. Although the example is based on a transaction resulting in the formation of a joint venture, the accounting treatment by party A would be the same if it had obtained an interest in an associate. Illustration 7-10: Contribution of non-monetary assets to form a joint venture with cash equalisation payment between venturers/investors Suppose that the transaction in Illustration 7-9 above was varied so that A has a 36% interest in JV Co. However, as shown by the introductory table in Illustration 7-9 above, A is contributing intangible assets worth 40% of the total fair value of JV Co. Accordingly, B makes good the shortfall by making a cash payment to A - equivalent to 4% of the fair value of JV Co, i.e., CU10m (4% of CU250m). This would require A to make the following accounting entries. CU m CU m Dr Investment in JV Co: • Share of net identifiable assets of JV Co (1) 64.8 • Goodwill (2) 10.8 Dr Cash (equalisation payment from B) 10.0 Cr Intangible assets contributed to JV Co (3) 60.0 Cr Gain on disposal (4) 25.6 (1) 36% of fair value of separable net identifiable assets of new entity CU79.2m (36% of [CU100m + CU120m] as in table in Illustration 7-9 above) less elimination of 36% of gain on disposal CU14.4m (36% of CU40m, being the difference between the book value [CU60m] and fair value [CU100m] of A’s intangible assets, as in table in Illustration 7-9 above, contributed to JV Co) = CU64.8m. This is equivalent to, and perhaps more easily calculated as, 36% of [book value of A’s intangible assets + fair value of B’s separable net identifiable assets], i.e., 36% × [CU60m + CU120m] = CU64.8m. Under the equity method, this CU64.8m together with the CU10.8m of goodwill (see (2) below) would be included as the equity accounted amount of JV Co. Investments in associates and joint ventures (2) Fair value of consideration given CU100m (as in table in Illustration 7-9 above), less cash equalisation payment received CU10m = CU90m less fair value of 36% share of separable net identifiable assets of JV Co acquired CU79.2m (see (1) above) = CU10.8m. This is equivalent to, and perhaps more easily calculated as, 36% of the fair value of B’s goodwill, i.e., 36% × CU30m = CU10.8m. Under the equity method, as noted at (1) above, this CU10.8m together with the CU64.8m relating to the separable net identifiable assets would be included as the equity accounted amount of JV Co. (3) Previous carrying amount of intangible assets contributed by A, now deconsolidated. (4) Fair value of business acquired CU90m (36% of CU250m) plus cash equalisation payment CU10m = CU100m, less book value of intangible assets disposed of CU60m (as in table in Illustration 7-9 above) = CU40m, less 36% of gain eliminated (CU14.4m) = CU25.6m. The CU14.4m eliminated reduces A’s share of JV Co’s separable net identifiable assets by CU14.4m (see (1) above). 7.5.3 Conflict between IAS 28 and IFRS 10 In Illustration 7-9 above, we considered the accounting by Entity A when the non-monetary assets it has contributed are intangible assets that are not in a subsidiary. Entity B has contributed an interest in a subsidiary that is a business. The accounting for Entity B is subject to the conflict between IFRS 10 and IAS 28, as described at section 6.2.2 above and in more detail in Chapter 8 at section 3.3.2. Consequently, Entity B has a policy choice between full and partial gain recognition described at section 6.2.2.B above. In Illustration 7-11 below, we illustrate how Entity B, which has contributed a subsidiary that constitutes a business in return for its interest in the joint venture in the transaction set out in Illustration 7-9 above, would account for the transaction by applying the requirements of IFRS 10. I.e., Entity B has elected to apply IFRS 10 to the loss of control transaction even though it is a downstream transaction. Although, the example is based on a transaction resulting in the formation of a joint venture, the accounting treatment by Entity B would be the same if it had obtained an interest in an associate. Illustration 7-11: Contribution of subsidiary to form a joint venture - applying IFRS 10 A and B are two major pharmaceutical companies, which agree to form a joint venture (JV Co). A will own 40% of the joint venture, and B 60%. The parties agree that the total value of the new business of JV Co is CU250m. A’s contribution to the venture is several intangible assets, with a carrying amount of CU60m. The fair value of the intangible assets contributed by A is CU100m, i.e., equivalent to 40% of the total fair value of JV Co of CU250m. B contributes a subsidiary that is a business with separable net identifiable assets of CU85m and goodwill of CU15m. The fair value of the separable net identifiable assets is CU120m. The implicit fair value of the business contributed is CU150m (60% of total fair value of JV Co of CU250m). The book and fair values of the assets/businesses contributed by A and B can therefore be summarised as follows: A B (in CU m) Book value Fair value Book value Fair value Intangible assets Separable net identifiable assets Goodwill Total Applying IFRS 10 to the transaction results in B reflecting the following accounting entry. CU m CU m Dr Investment in JV Co: • Share of net identifiable assets of JV Co (1) • Goodwill (2) Cr Separable net identifiable assets and goodwill contributed to JV Co (3) Cr Gain on disposal (4) Investments in associates and joint ventures (1) 60% of fair value of separable net identifiable assets of new entity CU132m (60% of [CU100m + CU120m] as in table above). There is no elimination of 60% of the gain on disposal. Under the equity method, this CU132m together with the CU18m of goodwill (see (2) below) would be included as the equity accounted amount of JV Co. (2) Fair value of consideration given of CU60m (being 40% of CU150m as in table above) plus fair value of retained interest of CU90m (being 60% of CU150m) less fair value of 60% share of separable net identifiable assets of JV Co acquired CU132m (see (1) above). Under the equity method, as noted at (1) above, this CU18m together with the CU132m relating to the separable net identifiable assets would be included as the equity accounted amount of JV Co. (3) Previous carrying amount of net identifiable assets contributed by B as in table above, now deconsolidated. There would be a number of entries to deconsolidate these on a line-by-line basis. (4) Fair value of consideration received of CU60m (being 60% of CU100m as in table above) plus fair value of retained interest of CU90m (being 60% of CU150m) less book value of assets disposed of CU100m (see (3) above) = CU50m. 7.5.4 Accounting for the acquisition of a business on formation of a joint venture IFRS 3 does not apply to business combinations that arise on the formation of a joint venture. [IFRS 3.2(a)]. Therefore, it is not clear under IFRS accounting standards how the acquisition by a joint venture of the former business of a venturer is accounted for in the financial statements of the joint venture itself. How we see it We believe that under the GAAP hierarchy in IAS 8 the pooling of interests method is available when accounting for the businesses acquired on the formation of a joint venture. However, there are other acceptable approaches (including the acquisition method) that give a fair presentation in particular circumstances. When a new company is formed to create a joint venture and both venturers contribute a business, we believe that it would also be acceptable under the GAAP hierarchy in IAS 8.11 (see Chapter 3 at section 4.3) to apply the acquisition method to both businesses, as IFRS accounting standards do not prevent entities from doing this and it provides useful information to investors. The entity makes disclosures that are sufficient for users of the financial statements to understand the transaction fully. In Illustration 7-9 above, if JV Co were to apply the acquisition method, it could mean that the equity-accounted amounts included in the financial statements of B bear little relation to its share of the net assets of the joint venture as reported in the underlying financial statements of the investee. This would be the case if B accounted for the transaction by applying IAS 28 rather than IFRS 10 (see section 7.5.3 above). For example, B’s share of any amortisation charge recorded by JV Co must be based on the carrying amount of B’s share of JV Co’s intangible assets, not as recorded in JV Co’s books (i.e., at fair value) but as recorded in B’s books, which will be based on book value for intangible assets contributed by B and at fair value for intangible assets contributed by A. Accordingly it would be necessary for B to keep a ‘memorandum’ set of books for equity accounting purposes reflecting its share of assets that were originally its own at book value and those originally of A at fair value. In Illustration 7-9 above, JV Co must account for the intangibles contributed by A at fair value because the transaction represents a share-based payment transaction in terms of IFRS 2 Share-based Payment. Therefore, A will need to keep a ‘memorandum’ record relating to these intangibles, so that it can make the necessary adjustments in its equity accounting to reflect amortisation charges based on its original book values. Alternatively, if JV Co were to apply the pooling of interests method and therefore reflect assets contributed from both A and B at their book values, A would need to keep a ‘memorandum’ set of books for equity accounting purposes because its share of assets that were originally those of B are carried at fair value rather than carry-over cost. Investments in associates and joint ventures 7.5.5 Contributions to an associate that is under common control Frequently asked questions Question 7-1: How does an entity account for a contribution of PP&E to a newly formed associate in exchange for shares in the associate when the entity and the fellow investors in the associate are entities under common control? In January 2018, the IFRS IC was asked how an entity accounts for a transaction in which it contributes PP&E to a newly formed associate in exchange for shares in the associate. In the fact pattern described in the request, the entity and the fellow investors in the associate are entities under common control. The investors each contribute items of PP&E to the new entity in exchange for shares in that entity. The IFRS IC firstly observed that unless a standard specifically excludes common control transactions from its scope, an entity applies the applicable requirements in the standard to common control transactions.
